L347: ## **Efficacy Subgroup Analyses**
L348: The Applicant performed multiple subgroup analyses for the primary endpoint, including age, ppFEV1 at baseline, SwCl at baseline, sex, and geographic region. Overall, the subgroups evaluations were generally consistent with the overall population. Select subgroup analyses are summarized in **Figure 2**.
L349: **Figure 2. Subgroup Analyses of Change From Baseline in ppFEV1 Through Week 24 (FAS), Trial 102**
L350: Source: Statistical analyst. Software: R. adsl and adsp datasets.
L351: Abbreviations: CI, confidence interval; D-IVA, deutivacaftor; ELX, elexacaftor; FAS, full analysis set; IVA, ivacaftor; LS, least squares; n, number of subjects in subset; ppFEV1, percent predicted forced expiratory volume in 1 second; SwCl, sweat chloride; TEZ, tezacaftor; VNZ, vanzacaftor
L352: For the adolescent subgroup (subjects with an age at screening of &lt;18 years), the LS mean difference of the ppFEV1 between VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA and ELX/TEZ/IVA treatment groups was -1.3% (95% CI: -3.9%, 1.3%). The point estimate numerically favors ELX/TEZ/IVA treatment and the lower bound of the 95% CI is smaller than the NI margin of -3.0%. Interpretation of results in the adolescent subgroup is limited by the wide 95% CI, likely because of the small sample size.
L353: In subjects with a SwCl of &lt;30mmol/L at baseline, the 95% confidence interval of the LS mean difference in ppFEV1 between the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA and ELX/TEZ/IVA treatment groups ruled out the NI margin and excluded the null. However, the interpretation of this finding is limited by a small sample size, as it represents less than 10% of the overall population; therefore, the clinical meaning of this finding is unclear.
L354: ## 6.2.3. Trial VX20-121-103 (Trial 103)
L355: ## 6.2.3.1. Design, Trial 103
L356: The overall design and endpoint selection for Trial 103 were the same as Trial 102 (reviewed in Section 6.2.2.1 and Section 15.1). The dosages evaluated in Trial 103 were also the same as Trial 102. The Applicant proposed a noninferiority margin of absolute change in FEV1 of -3.0% supported by data from the ELX/TEZ/IVA development program that included a similar rationale as for Trial 102:
L357: - For the population eligible for Trial 103 (F/F and others), there were no historical placebo-controlled trials to derive a NI margin by the usual approach. The Applicant employed a novel method based on Bucher's network indirect comparison approach to come up with 12% as M1 (Bucher et al. 1997). Then similarly, the Applicant proposed 3.0% as the NI margin, and the Division generally agreed with the proposal, acknowledging the lack of historical placebo-controlled data. See Section 15.1 for additional information on the NI margin.
L358: As in Trial 102, all other efficacy endpoints were analyzed using superiority comparisons, some of which were controlled for multiplicity (refer to Section 15.1 for details).
L359: ## 6.2.3.2. Eligibility Criteria, Trial 103
L360: The eligibility criteria for Trial 103 were also very similar to Trial 102 (reviewed in Section 6.2.2.1 and Section 15.1) with the exception of the *CFTR* variant based inclusion criteria.
L361: Trial 103 enrolled subjects with the following *CFTR* genotypes:
L362: - Homozygous for *F508del* (F/F)
- Heterozygous for *F508del* and a gating variant (F/G)
- Heterozygous for *F508del* and a residual function variant (F/RF)
- At least one other TCR *CFTR* variant identified as responsive to ELZ/TEZ/IVA and no *F508del* variant (TCR/non-F)
L363: G variants and RF variants, also referred to as conduction variants, are classified as pathologic *CFTR* variants in which CFTR protein is created and trafficked to the cell surface but chloride transport through the protein channel is decreased because the channel does not open or function properly (CFF 2024). A summary of the G and RF variants eligible for Trial 102 provided by the Applicant is included in Section 15.1. Note there can be overlap of certain variants between these two CFTR classification groups.
L364: ## 6.2.3.3. Statistical Analysis Plan, Trial 103
L365: The statistical analysis plan for Trial 103 was the same as Trial 102 (reviewed in Section 6.2.2.3 and Section 15.1), with the exception of sample size that was powered at 95% for Trial 103 compared to 90% for Trial 102.
L366: ## 6.2.3.4. Results of Analyses, Trial 103
L367: ## Subject Enrollment and Disposition
L368: A total of 597 subjects were enrolled in Trial 103. During the run-in period, 24 (4.0%) subjects discontinued the trial. The primary reason listed for trial discontinuation was “other” (11 subjects, 1.8%) and included reasons such as subject preference and antibiotic requirements for CF pulmonary exacerbations. Following the run-in period, 574 subjects were randomized and
L369: 573 (99.8%) received at least 1 dose of the trial drug and are considered the FAS. A summary of the subject enrollment and disposition is included in Table 21.

L374: As with Trial 102, the majority of subjects in Trial 103 completed treatment (532, 92.8%) and the trial (543, 94.8%). There were numerically more subjects in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA group that discontinued both treatment (25, 8.8%) and the trial (20, 7.0%) than in the ELX/TEZ/IVA group (discontinued treatment: 16, 5.5%; discontinued trial: 10, 3.5%), as shown in Table 21. The main reason listed for treatment and trial discontinuation was adverse events, reviewed in more detail in Section 7.6.1.4. This numerical difference is unlikely to affect the analysis given the overall low rates of discontinuation. The trial completion and discontinuation rates were similar to Trial 102 and other phase 3 programs in this population.

The baseline average SwCl in Trial 103 is lower than in Trial 102, which is reflective of the differences in *CFTR* variant eligibility criteria. L405: ## Analysis of Efficacy – Primary Endpoint
L406: Trial 103 was also designed as noninferiority trial with a designated margin of -3.0%. Through Week 24, treatment with VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA was also noninferior to ELX/TEZ/IVA in the absolute change from baseline in ppFEV1, the primary efficacy endpoint. The LS mean difference was 0.2% (95% CI: -0.5%, 0.9%) in the FAS population, as shown in Table 26.

The robustness of the analysis result regarding missing data was supported by the tipping point sensitivity analysis. See Section 16.2 for additional information.

Overall, the subgroups evaluations were generally consistent with the overall population. Select subgroup analyses are summarized in Figure 3.

L441: ## 6.2.4. Trial VX21-121-105 (Trial 105)
L442: ## 6.2.4.1. Design, Trial 105 (Cohorts A1 and B1)
L443: Trial 105 was a phase 3, 2-part (Parts A and B), multicenter, multicohort, open-label trial in pediatric CF subjects. Cohorts A1 and B1 enrolled subjects 6 through 11 years of age and the final analysis results are presented below. The remainder of the cohorts in Trial 105 are either ongoing or have not yet begun enrollment. Ultimately, Trial 105 will enroll CF subjects 1 through 11 years of age (inclusive).
L444: ## **Part A (PK)**
L445: There was a 28-day screening period followed by a 15-day treatment period. A review of safety, tolerability, and available PK data was completed by the Applicant after Cohort A1 completed treatment to determine the doses and weight cutoffs for Cohort B1.
L446: ## Primary Endpoints
L447: - PK parameters of VNZ, TEZ, D-IVA, and relevant metabolites
- Safety and tolerability as determined by AEs, clinical laboratory values, standard 12-lead electrocardiograms, vital signs, and pulse oximetry
L448: All subjects in Cohort A1 received VNZ 10 mg QD/TEZ 50 mg QD/D-IVA 125 mg QD.
L449: ## **Part B (Safety and Tolerability)**
L450: Part B also included a 28-day screening period with a 28-day ELX/TEZ/IVA run-in period to establish an on-treatment (ELX/TEZ/IVA) baseline. The run-in period was waived for subjects already receiving stable ELX/TEZ/IVA prior to enrollment. The screening and run-in (if applicable) periods were followed by a 24 Week treatment period, during which all subjects received VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA.
L451: The primary endpoint of Part B was safety and tolerability of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. Additional efficacy endpoints from Trials 102 and 103 were examined in exploratory analyses. A full review of the trial protocol design, trial population, objectives, and endpoints is included in Section 15.2. In general, the trial design and population were consistent with previous trials in the CF pediatric population for other similar medications.
L452: The doses of trial drug for Cohort B1 are reviewed in Table 29.
L453: **Table 29. Run-In and Treatment Period Doses, Trial 105 (Cohort B1)**
L454: Cohort B1 Run-In Period

≥30 kg	200 mg QD	100 mg QD	150 mg q12h

L456: Abbreviations: D-IVA, deutivacaftor; ELX, elaxacaftor; IVA, ivacaftor; q12h, dose every 12 hours; QD, once daily; TEZ, tezacaftor; VNZ, vanzacaftor
L457: ## **6.2.4.2. Eligibility Criteria, Trial 105**
L458: The eligibility criteria for Cohorts A1 and B1 of Trial 105 were reasonable for a stable CF population aged 6 through 11 years. The *CFTR*-variant based eligibility included at least one TCR variant, including *F508del*, in the *CFTR* gene.
L459: The key inclusion criteria included:
L460: - Ages 6 through 11 years (inclusive) on the date of informed consent
- Weight between 5th and 9th percentile (inclusive) for weight-for-age at screening
- At least one TCR *CFTR* variant, including *F508del*
- FEV_1 \ge 60\% predicted at screening
- Stable CF disease judged by the investigator
L461: The key exclusion criteria included:
L462: - History of intolerance to trial drug including ELX/TEZ/IVA
- History of comorbidity including but not limited to hepatic cirrhosis, moderate to severe hepatic impairment, chronic kidney disease, solid organ, or hematological transplantation
- Acute upper or lower respiratory tract infection, infectious PEx of CF, or changes in therapy (including antibiotics) for sinopulmonary disease
L463: The trial population in Cohorts A1 and B1 of Trial 105 were reasonable and similar to other pediatric CF development programs.
L464: ## 6.2.4.3. Statistical Analysis Plan, Trial 105
L465: Since Cohort A1 was for PK assessment, the statistical analysis plan is described for Cohort B1. For change from baseline endpoints, a MMRM approach was employed. The primary result obtained from the model was the average treatment effect through Week 24. The estimated mean change from baseline through Week 24 along with the corresponding 2-sided 95% CI were provided. For the number of events variables, a descriptive summary along with the observed event rate was provided. The number and proportion of subjects with SwCl &lt;60mmol/L was descriptively summarized with the 2-sided 95% CI based on Clopper-Pearson method.
L466: ## 6.2.4.4. Results of Analyses, Trial 105
L467: ## Subject Enrollment and Disposition
L468: In Trial 105, Cohort A1 enrolled 17 subjects and all subjects completed treatment and the trial cohort. Cohort B1 enrolled 78 subjects: 17 (21.8%) entered the treatment period after completion of the ELX/TEZ/IVA run-in period, and the remaining 61 (78.2%) subjects directly entered the treatment period following screening, since they were on stable commercial ELX/TEZ/IVA for at least 28 days prior to screening.
L469: Cohort B1 enrolled 78 subjects and all subjects completed the trial. The majority of subjects also completed treatment with VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA with the exception of 1 (1.3%) subject who discontinued treatment due to an adverse event (reviewed in Section 7.6.2.4). The rates of completion of the trial and treatment were consistent with other similarly designed trials in this
L470: population. The majority of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA exposure occurred in Cohort B1, and this will be the focus of the remainder of this section of the review unless otherwise noted.

L506: ## 6.2.4.5. Pediatric (6 Through 11 Years of Age) Efficacy Summary
L507: The PK data for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA administration, reviewed in Section 14.2.6, demonstrated that systemic exposures were comparable to those observed in the adolescent and adult population. Because of the similar systemic exposures and because the disease process in the older population is the same as that in the 6- to 11-year-old population, efficacy in this age group was extrapolated from the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA adolescent and adult development program.
L508: ## 6.3. Key Efficacy Review Issues
L509: ## 6.3.1. Satisfaction of 21 CFR 300.50, the “Combination Rule”
L510: ## Issue
L511: VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA is a FDC drug product. The Applicant has proposed to use a combination of clinical and in vitro data to support satisfaction of 21 CFR 300.50, i.e., to demonstrate the contribution of each active ingredient to the claimed effects of the combination product.
L512: ## Background
L513: The regulatory evidentiary standard to support approval for a new fixed drug combination product must consider 21 CFR 300.50(a), often referred to as the “combination rule,” which states that, “two or more drugs may be combined in a single dosage form when each component makes a contribution to the claimed effect and the dosage of each...is such that the combination is safe and effective for a significant patient population requiring such concurrent therapy...”.
L514: Often, the Agency relies on factorial design clinical trials to evaluate and support the contribution of each component in the combination product. However, there is no single regulatory framework for satisfying the combination rule, and the Agency may exercise flexibility, particularly in cases when the beneficial treatment effect of a particular drug class is well understood and established.
L515: During the development of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA, the Applicant proposed to leverage a combination of in vitro and clinical data to demonstrate the individual contributions of VNZ, TEZ, and D-IVA to the efficacy of the FDC product, rather than rely on a full factorial design approach. The Division agreed this approach was reasonable. There is regulatory precedent for this approach, which is similar to that used to support the approval of ELX/TEZ/IVA.
L516: ## Assessment
L517: The Division completed a multidisciplinary review of the clinical and in vitro data to evaluate the contributions of VNZ, TEZ, and D-IVA, which included the various types of evidence described below. For additional details of the clinical pharmacology assessment, refer to Section 14.
L518: ## The Mechanism of Action or Action Site
L519: - VNZ and TEZ both facilitate the cellular processing and trafficking of select *CFTR* variants (including *F508del-CFTR*). The binding sites of VNZ and TEZ on the CFTR protein are different, and in vitro pharmacology results demonstrate that VNZ and TEZ work synergistically.
- Similar to IVA, D-IVA is a CFTR potentiator that increases the CFTR channel open probability at the cell surface and facilitates increased chloride ion transport.
L520: APPEARS THIS WAY ON
ORIGINAL
L521: ## The Contribution of D-IVA in the Treatment of CF
L522: - The D-IVA dose-ranging trial (Study VX18-561-101) demonstrated that D-IVA 150 mg or 250 mg QD resulted in a sustained ppFEV1 response over 12 weeks in CF subjects with gating CFTR variants who were responsive to and prescribed a stable dose of IVA monotherapy. In addition, the ppFEV1 change from baseline values following 12-week treatment with D-IVA 150 mg or 250 mg QD were comparable to CF subjects who were treated with IVA monotherapy for 12 weeks. The results indicate the similar treatment effect between D-IVA and IVA on a clinically meaningful endpoint.
- The following in vitro electrophysiology studies demonstrated the similar CFTR potentiating potencies between D-IVA and IVA:Study PH-088 demonstrated that D-IVA and IVA have similar effects in increasing *G551D/F508del* CFTR chloride transport in HBE cells.
- Study PH-045 demonstrated that D-IVA and IVA have similar effects in increasing *G551D* CFTR chloride transport in FRT cells.
- Study PH-043 demonstrated that D-IVA and IVA have similar effects in increasing *F508del/F508del* CFTR chloride transport in HBE cells.
- Study Q-142 demonstrated that D-IVA and IVA have almost identical concentration-response relationship to increase *F508del/F508del* and *F508del/minimal function (MF)* CFTR chloride transport when in combination with IVA and TEZ in HBE cells.
L523: ## The Contribution of TEZ in the Treatment of CF
L524: - In the development program of TEZ/IVA (NDA 210491), Trial VX14-661-108 demonstrated that TEZ 100 mg QD/IVA 150 mg q12h was superior in improving ppFEV1 from baseline compared to IVA 150 mg q12h monotherapy in CF subjects with *F508del/residual function* variants. The results indicate the contribution of TEZ in treatment of CF on IVA background therapy on a clinically meaningful endpoint.
- The following in vitro electrophysiology studies demonstrated the contribution of TEZ in increasing CFTR chloride transport:Factorial Study N035 demonstrated synergistic effect between TEZ and IVA where a greater *F508del/F508del* CFTR chloride transport was observed with TEZ/IVA compared to TEZ or IVA alone in HBE cells. In addition, both TEZ or IVA alone increased chloride transport when compared to vehicle control.
- Study S525 demonstrated an increase of *F508del/F508del* and *F508del/MF* variant CFTR chloride transport with TEZ/VNZ in HBE cells compared to VNZ alone, indicating the additional contribution of TEZ on background VNZ treatment.
L525: ## The Contribution of VNZ in the Treatment of CF
L526: - In the development program of ELX/TEZ/IVA (NDA 212273), the phase 3 trial (VX17-445-103) demonstrated that ELX/TEZ/IVA is superior compared to TEZ/IVA with respect to the absolute change from baseline in ppFEV1 at Week 4 in *F508del* homozygous subjects. This supports the contribution of ELX to the full efficacy of ELX/TEZ/IVA.
- In VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA program, Trials 102 and 103 demonstrated noninferiority of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA to ELX/TEZ/IVA as measured by the absolute change from baseline in ppFEV1 through Week 24. The results from these trials, taken together, indicate the necessity of VNZ to contribute to the full efficacy of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA because the dose of TEZ is the same between VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA and ELX/TEZ/IVA and because D-IVA 250 mg QD and IVA 150 mg q12h have a similar treatment effect (see above).
- The following in vitro electrophysiology studies demonstrated the contribution of VNZ in the increase in CFTR chloride transport:Study S525 demonstrated a concentration-response relationship for VNZ to increase *F508del/F508del* and *F508del/MF* variant CFTR chloride transport in HBE cells.
- Study S525 demonstrated an increase of *F508del/F508del* and *F508del/MF* variant CFTR chloride transport with VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA compared to TEZ/IVA in HBE cells.
- Study U020 demonstrated that VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA and ELX/TEZ/IVA have similar effects increasing *N1303K/N1303K* and *N1303K/minimal function* variant CFTR chloride transport in HBE cells, and both triple combinations are superior to effect of TEZ/IVA.
L527: ## Conclusion
L528: The following data sources demonstrate the contribution of each component of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA to the effect of the combination product: the demonstration of noninferiority of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA to ELX/TEZ/IVA in two phase 3 clinical trials; the available historical clinical trial data with IVA, TEZ/IVA, and ELX/TEZ/IVA; the support of electrophysiology in vitro data. The Division concludes that there is adequate data from the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA development program to satisfy the combination rule (21 CFR 300.50).
L529: ## 6.3.2. Clinical Relevance of Sweat Chloride Concentration Efficacy Endpoints
L530: ## Issue
L531: SwCl concentrations are pharmacodynamic measurements of CFTR function. The phase 3 development program for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA included secondary endpoints related to SwCl, including the change from baseline in SwCl and responder analyses for the proportion of subjects with SwCl below 60mmol/L and 30mmol/L, which are used clinically as the CF diagnostic threshold and the normal range threshold, respectively (Farrell et al. 2017). Most results of the SwCl analyses were statistically significant, favoring VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA compared to ELX/TEZ/IVA; however, the clinical meaningfulness of the SwCl results is not established at this time.
L532: ## **Background**
L533: SwCl concentration measurements have been secondary endpoints in multiple clinical development programs for CF, including ELX/TEZ/IVA. The Division views SwCl as an important pharmacodynamic measurement for drugs that act on CFTR. However, as SwCl measurements do not reflect how a patient feels, functions, or survives, they are not considered clinically meaningful endpoints, nor have they been validated as surrogate endpoints. (b) (4)
L534: (see Section 6.2.2).
L535: SwCl concentrations are part of the CF diagnostic algorithm (Farrell et al. 2017) but are not used clinically as prognostic or predictive markers. There are no clinical care guidelines that recommend serial SwCl measurements after diagnosis of CF or recommend repeating SwCl measurements following initiation of therapy to assess response to intervention. Therefore, information about post-treatment SwCl is of unclear meaning to prescribers and patients.
L536: (b) (4) the Applicant cited data regarding the natural history of CF, drawn primarily from the U.S. Cystic Fibrosis Foundation Patient Registry, which suggests SwCl levels at diagnosis may be prognostic for clinical outcomes, including rate of pulmonary function decline and mortality (McKone et al. 2015). However, other studies that have evaluated whether SwCl may predict severity of CF phenotype have shown inconsistent results (Wilschanski et al. 1995; Davis et al. 2004). In addition, there is no evidence to support that post-treatment SwCl concentrations will have predictive value for clinically meaningful outcomes, such as rate of lung function decline or mortality.
L537: We note that the clinical development program for ELX/TEZ/IVA established a large and sustained treatment effect as measured by the ppFEV1 for the overall phase 3 population enrolled in the registrational trials. In a postmarket study that evaluated both the change in ppFEV1 and change in SwCl six months after initiation of ELX/TEZ/IVA, there was a wide range of variability in the correlation between outcomes, i.e., subjects with minimal SwCl changes with a large FEV1 response and subjects with large SwCl changes with a minimal FEV1 response (Nichols et al. 2022). These data suggest that reliance on changes in SwCl alone will not predict clinically meaningful outcomes.
L538: Additional published data from an observational study comparing pre- and post-ELX/TEZ/IVA SwCl levels also demonstrate heterogeneity in SwCl response to treatment. This study included variations in post-ELX/TEZ/IVA SwCl levels between *CFTR* genotype groupings (e.g., *F508del/MF* versus *F508del/F508del*, in which patients had one versus two ELX/TEZ/IVA responsive variants, respectively) as well as variation in response within a *CFTR* genotype grouping (Zemanick et al. 2024). These data further limit the potential for SwCl as a predictive marker in this context.
L539: ## **Assessment**
L540: Trials 102 and 103 included multiple key and other secondary endpoints related to SwCl concentration measurements. A summary of baseline SwCl measurements in the trial population and SwCl-based secondary endpoint results are included in Table 34.
L541: **Table 34. Sweat Chloride Endpoints and Baseline Measurements (FAS), Trials 102 and 103**

L544: Abbreviations: CI, confidence interval; CFB, change from baseline; D-IVA, deutivacaftor; ELX, elexacaftor; FAS, full analysis set; IVA, ivacaftor; n, number of subjects in subset; OR, odds ratio; SD, standard deviation; SwCl, sweat chloride; TEZ, tezacaftor; VNZ, vanzacaftor; vs., versus; W, week
L545: ## Change From Baseline
L546: As shown in Table 34, treatment with VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA led to a statistically significant reduction in SwCl concentration from baseline compared to treatment with ELX/TEZ/IVA in both phase 3 trials, with mean reductions in SwCl from baseline of -8.4mmol/L and -2.8mmol/L in Trials 102 and 103, respectively. The clinical meaningfulness of this change is unclear. Since SwCl is not a clinically meaningful endpoint, there is no established threshold for quantifying what magnitude of change may be clinically significant, nor has the Applicant proposed such a threshold. However, we note that the changes observed are numerically small. The difference in the magnitude of change between the two trials is also of unclear significance and may be a function of genotypic differences in SwCl at baseline, as discussed more below. The Division maintains that these results provide pharmacodynamic information to prescribers demonstrating comparable on-targets effects of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA and ELX/TEZ/IVA (b) (4)
L547: ## Responder Analyses &lt;60mmol/L and &lt;30mmol/L
L548: The Applicant also proposed the use of pooled responder analyses (b) (4)
L549: As shown in Table 34, subjects treated with VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA were more likely to have a SwCl concentration &lt;60mmol/L or &lt;30mmol/L compared to ELX/TEZ/IVA at 24 weeks, based on pooled responder analyses. These threshold values are based on the CF diagnostic threshold (60mmol/L) and normal SwCl range (&lt;30mmol/L). As discussed above, we do not agree that the thresholds proposed by the Applicant are clinically meaningful—these
L550: thresholds are valid for diagnostic purposes, but their utility in measuring response to treatment is not clear or established within the CF literature.
L551: Furthermore, the Division does not agree that the Applicant's decision to pool data for the responder analyses from Trials 102 and 103 was clinically appropriate. The populations in Trials 102 and 103 had different *CFTR* variant-based eligibility criteria (see Section 15.1). Trial 102 enrolled subjects heterozygous for *F508del* and an MF variant (i.e., subjects with only one variant predicted to be responsive to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA), whereas Trial 103 enrolled a wide range of *CFTR* genotypes and included many subjects with two responsive variants (i.e., subjects homozygous for *F508del* or heterozygous for *F508del* and another response variant). The available data support that ELX/TEZ/IVA and VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA are clinically effective in patients with one or two responsive variants as measured by FEV_1; however, since SwCl is a pharmacodynamic measurement of CFTR function, differences in both the number and type of *CFTR* variant may result in significant discrepancies and heterogeneity in SwCl levels, as supported by the CF literature and observations from the trial results (Zemanick et al. 2024). The baseline SwCl levels were different between the populations of the two trials with a mean baseline SwCl of 53.0mmol/L and 42.8mmol/L in Trials 102 and 103, respectively. This difference was, in part, attributable to baseline CFTR genotypic differences. As such, we observed that there was a greater proportion of subjects in Trial 102 with a baseline SwCl \ge 60mmol/L compared to Trial 103 (140 [35.2%] and 100 [17.5%] subjects, respectively) and a lower proportion of subjects with a baseline SwCl &lt;30mmol/L (36 [9.0%] and 152 [26.5%] subjects, respectively). Thus, as expected, as shown in Table 34, the proportion of responders in Trial 102 is greater than Trial 103, an important distinction that is lost in the pooled analysis. The genotype differences and expected response to therapy between the enrolled populations of the two phase 3 trials do not support pooling of the data.
L552: We also do not agree that pooling these results was appropriate from a statistical perspective in the context of two adequate and well controlled trials submitted to support substantial evidence of effectiveness. (b) (4)
L553: There was no justification from the Applicant for pooling this endpoint based on statistical considerations. Although the Applicant also included the responder analyses for the trials individually as other secondary endpoints, these were conducted outside of multiplicity control. In these exploratory analyses, results were generally consistent with the other SwCl based secondary endpoints but remain of unclear clinical significance.
L554: The Applicant's statistical handling of the responder analyses, pooled and unpoooled, included all subjects in the analysis, whether the individual's baseline SwCl was above or below the specified threshold. Only a limited number of subjects had baseline SwCl values &gt;60mmol/L in both trials, as shown in Table 34. The Applicant partially addressed this issue by accounting for baseline imbalance via the statistical analysis model; however, we note this as an additional limitation to the interpretability of the results.
L555: ## Conclusion
L556: The change from baseline SwCl results provide (b) (4)
L557: pharmacodynamic information on CFTR function and will be communicated in the USPI with appropriate language to convey the uncertainty of the clinical meaning.(b) (4)
L558: ## 6.3.3. Additional Data Sources to Support Indication Based on *CFTR* Variant
L559: ## Issue
L560: The Applicant submitted multiple types of data, including clinical trial results, in vitro models, and scientific justification for extrapolation, to support the inclusion of a total of 303 *CFTR* variants in the indication for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA.
L561: ## Background
L562: There are over 700 variants in the *CFTR* gene that are associated with CF disease causation, although over 2,000 variants have been identified (CFF; JHU; and HSC 2024). The most common CF-causing variant in the United States is *F508del*. Approximately 85% of individuals with genotype information available are heterozygous for *F508del* and 44% are *F508del* homozygous (CFF 2023). The reported prevalence of the next most reported *CFTR* variant in the United States is significantly lower with no other variant reported in &gt;5.0% of the population in 2023 (CFF 2023).
L563: Given the rarity of many *CFTR* variants, relying on clinical trial data alone to demonstrate efficacy is not feasible. In this context, in vitro data have supported the expansion of the indication to additional *CFTR* variants for ELX/TEZ/IVA and other medications that contain one or more of its components. The pathophysiology of CF is well understood, and the in vitro data used has primarily been from an in vitro Ussing chamber assays using the FRT cell system to model electrophysiologic changes (Durmowicz et al. 2018). Recently, in vitro HBE cells have also been used to support the effectiveness of ELX/TEZ/IVA by demonstrating increased chloride transport in the *N1303K* variant, which was not responsive to ELX/TEZ/IVA when expressed in the FRT system (refer to the supplemental NDAs 212273/S012 and 217660/S003 uploaded to DARRTS on December 11, 2024 [DARRTS ID: 5493844]).
L564: In addition, there remain *CFTR* variants that are not amenable to evaluation within these in vitro models and are considered too rare to be easily evaluated in a clinical trial. Some of these variants, including 16 ultra-rare noncanonical splice (NCS) variants, which are expected to result in the presence of wild-type CFTR protein in a lower quantity than normal, were recently added to the indication for ELX/TEZ/IVA based on the extrapolation of efficacy and safety from a clinical trial, in which three of these types of variants were enrolled (Trial VX21-445-124). Extrapolation was supported by the knowledge that patients with these variants share a “residual function” clinical phenotype with patients in the clinical trial and that this residual function phenotype results from expression of wild-type CFTR protein, for which there is in vitro FRT data demonstrating that ELX/TEZ/IVA robustly augments chloride transport (refer to the supplemental NDAs 212273/S012 and 217660/S003 uploaded to DARRTS on December 11, 2024 [DARRTS ID: 5493844]).
L565: ## Assessment
L566: The Applicant has proposed the inclusion of 303 *CFTR* variants in the indication for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. Refer to Table 2 in Section 1.2 and see Table 196 in Section 16.2.3 for additional details on the data sources used to support the individual *CFTR* variants included in the proposed indication for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA.
L567: ## Clinical Data
L568: The clinical data relies primarily on the demonstration of efficacy in subjects with at least one copy of the *F508del CFTR* variant, as established by the noninferiority of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA to ELX/TEZ/IVA and the prior demonstration of efficacy of ELX/TEZ/IVA.
L569: For the remaining 302 variants, the data sources are summarized as follows. There is clinical data for an additional 23 variants from Trials 102 and 103, for which there is also nonclinical electrophysiology support.
L570: ## FRT Data
L571: The Applicant submitted FRT data demonstrating an increase in chloride transport of \ge 10\% over baseline with VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment for 31 *CFTR* variants that were not responsive to ELX/TEZ/IVA. See Section 14.1.2 for more details.
L572: In addition, the Applicant submitted FRT data demonstrating a \ge 10\% over baseline in chloride transport with VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA to support 74 variants that were recently added to the indication for ELX/TEZ/IVA on December 11, 2024, based on the FRT data. The threshold of a \ge 10\% over baseline in chloride transport established during the review of existing in vitro FRT data was also leveraged to support a total of 154 *CFTR* variants previously demonstrated to be responsive to ELX/TEZ/IVA, TEZ/IVA, or IVA. The use of this existing FRT data was discussed with the Applicant during the development of VNZ/TEZ/IVA and the Division agreed this was a reasonable approach.
L573: Overall, the FRT data submitted and reference to previously available data are supportive of inclusion of the proposed *CFTR* variants in the indication for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA.
L574: ## HBE Data
L575: In vitro data using HBE cells were used to support the inclusion of a single rare variant, *N1303K*, in the indication for ELX/TEZ/IVA (refer to the supplemental NDAs 212273/S012 &amp; 217660/S03 uploaded to DARRTS on December 11, 2024 [DARRTS ID: 5493844]). In this instance, HBE cells were used instead of FRT because the *N1303K* variant is not responsive to ELX/TEZ/IVA in the FRT system (see P289v4). At the time, a regulatory threshold to indicate effectiveness for ELX/TEZ/IVA using HBE cells had been not previously established, and the measure of effectiveness was determined by comparing the response of ELX/TEZ/IVA in HBE cells homozygous and heterozygous for *N1303K* to the response of TEZ/IVA in *F508del/F508del* HBE cells.
L576: Similarly, VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA demonstrated an increase in chloride transport in homozygous and heterozygous *N1303K* HBE cells that was greater than the effect of TEZ/IVA on chloride
L577: transport in *F508del/F508del* HBE cells, supporting inclusion of *N1303K* in the indication for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. Refer to Section 14.1.1 for more details of the clinical pharmacology assessment.
L578: ## Extrapolation of Efficacy in Noncanonical Splice Variants
L579: The Applicant proposed that the efficacy of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA be extrapolated to include 19 NCS variants. Based on the available data, these variants result in expression of wild-type CFTR protein at the apical cell surface but at lower levels than normal.
L580: These variants were added to the indication for ELX/TEZ/IVA on December 11, 2024, and included three NCS variants (i.e., *2789+5G&gt;A*, *3272-26A&gt;G*, *3849+10kbC&gt;T*), for which the efficacy of ELX/TEZ/IVA was demonstrated in Trial VX21-445-124, and 16 ultra-rare NCS variants, for which the efficacy and safety of ELX/TEZ/IVA was extrapolated. This extrapolation was based on similarities in clinical phenotype (i.e., “residual function”) with the variants included in Trial VX21-445-124 and in vitro data demonstrating that ELX/TEZ/IVA augments wild-type CFTR function. Refer to Section 14.1.2 for additional details.
L581: The data used to support the efficacy of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA in these NCS variants included the demonstration of the overall clinical noninferiority of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA to ELX/TEZ/IVA with supportive in vitro FRT data demonstrating that VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA also augments wild-type CFTR function. The data support the extrapolation of efficacy for these 19 *CFTR* variants based on the conclusion that the expected response to and benefits of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA would translate to patients with these rare NCS variants.
L582: ## Conclusion
L583: Overall, the data submitted by the Applicant to support the efficacy of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA in patients with *CFTR* variants where clinical trial data is infeasible to study is sufficient to support their inclusion in the indication for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. Refer to Section 14 for additional details.
L584: ## 7. Safety (Risk and Risk Management)
L585: ## 7.1. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on Nonclinical Data
L586: The nonclinical program for NDA 218730 primarily focused on evaluation of the safety of the new molecular entity VNZ. The Applicant established a scientific bridge to leverage the nonclinical toxicity data from the IVA program (NDA 203188, Trade name: KALYDECO [tablet]) to characterize the safety profile of D-IVA. The potential risks or safety concerns based on nonclinical data for TEZ were previously reviewed in the TEZ/IVA (NDA 210491, Trade name: SYMDEKO) and ELX/TEZ/IVA (NDA 212273, Trade name: TRIKAFTA [tablet]) programs. Overall, the nonclinical program supports the marketing application for NDA 218730.
L587: The nonclinical safety of VNZ was evaluated in safety pharmacology studies in the rat and dog, repeat dose toxicology studies in the rat and dog of up to 26 and 39 weeks duration, respectively;
L588: a full battery of developmental and reproductive toxicology studies in the rat and rabbit; genetic toxicology studies (Ames, in vitro micronucleus, and in vivo rat micronucleus assays); 6-month transgenic mouse carcinogenicity study; a juvenile toxicology study in rats; 13-week combination toxicology study in rats with VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA (see Section 13.1). A 2-year carcinogenicity study in rats will be conducted as a postmarketing requirement (see Section 24).
L589: In the general toxicology program for VNZ in rats, the major target organs of toxicity were the stomach (erosion/ulcers) and liver (hepatocellular vacuolar degeneration, multifocal hepatocellular necrosis) at exposures greater than 20 times (males) and 60 times (females) the exposure at the maximum recommended human dose (MRHD) of 20 mg VNZ. In the general toxicology program for VNZ in dogs, there were no potential safety concerns identified up to 100 times the exposure at the MRHD of VNZ. In a combination repeat dose toxicology study in rats, there were no adverse findings when VNZ, TEZ, and D-IVA were coadministered at doses that produced exposures 4, 1, and 1 times in male animals and 9, 2, and 1 times in female animals compared to the exposures at the MRHD, respectively. Safety pharmacology studies conducted in rats indicated that VNZ does not adversely affect the CNS or respiratory systems. The good laboratory practice human ether-a-go-go related gene assay indicated a potential QTc prolongation risk, which was not observed upon follow-up in the dog repeat dose toxicology studies. VNZ was negative for genotoxicity in the Ames assay, in vitro micronucleus assay in TK6 cells, and in vivo micronucleus assay in male Sprague-Dawley rats. In a 6-month transgenic mouse carcinogenicity study, there were no treatment-related tumor findings. Refer to Section 8.3 and Section 8.4 for an overview of juvenile and developmental and reproduction toxicity assessment with VNZ.
L590: D-IVA is the deuterated isotopologue of IVA. A scientific bridge was established between D-IVA and IVA in a 13-week repeat dose toxicology study in rats. In this study, the target organs of toxicity for both IVA and D-IVA were the kidney (basophilic tubules, tubular dilatation, and interstitial fibrosis), stomach (increased mucosal thickness), and thyroid (follicular cell hypertrophy). There were no toxicologically significant differences in findings between the same doses of D-IVA and IVA. Therefore, a bridge to the ivacaftor nonclinical program was justified and no further toxicology studies with D-IVA alone were recommended.
L591: ## 7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific Factors
L592: The VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA FDC is not included in a specific drug class. The established pharmaceutical class for D-IVA is a CFTR potentiator. The components VNZ and TEZ remain unclassified but facilitate the cellular processing and trafficking of defective CFTR protein to the cell surface.
L593: We focus this section of the review on ELX/TEZ/IVA because it is the active comparator in Trials 102 and 103 and shares components with VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. ELX/TEZ/IVA also represents the majority market share in prescriptions among this group of drugs, given its wider variant-based indication and relative greater efficacy (Heijerman et al. 2019; CFF 2023). In addition, the estimated cumulative worldwide patient exposure from marketing experience is also
L594: ## NDA 218730
## ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
L595: significantly higher for ELX/TEZ/IVA at 160,240 person-years, more than double other related medications (information provided by the Applicant).
L596: Recently a Boxed Warning related to drug-induced liver injury and liver failure was added to the United States Prescribing Information (USPI) of ELX/TEZ/IVA following the identification of cases of liver failure leading to death or transplantation in patients without a history of liver disease.5 The risk for drug-induced liver injury related to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA was a major focus of this safety review.
L597: In addition, the USPI of ELX/TEZ/IVA includes the following Warnings and Precautions6:
L598: - Hypersensitivity reactions, including anaphylaxis – as reported in postmarketing setting
- Concomitant use with cytochrome P450 (CYP)3A inducers – coadministration is not recommended
- Concomitant use with CYP3A inhibitors – the dose of ELX/TEZ/IVA should be reduced when used concomitantly with moderate or strong CYP3A inhibitors
- Cataracts – cases of noncongenital lens opacities have been reported in pediatric patients treated with IVA-containing regimens
L599: The most common adverse drug reactions listed in the USPI include headache, upper respiratory tract infection, abdominal pain, and diarrhea. Additional adverse reactions include rash, liver function test elevations, increased creatine phosphokinase, and increased blood pressure. The common adverse reactions were evaluated as part of the safety review of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA, and some were prespecified as adverse events of special interest (AESIs) (e.g., liver function testing elevations, increased creatine phosphokinase, rash events).
L600: In addition, there have been multiple postmarketing safety evaluations of ELX/TEZ/IVA and other similar medications as reviewed below in Table 35.

This safety signal assessment is in the action phase pending finalization at the time of this review. Postmarketing safety evaluations ongoing at the time of this review or ones that resulted in labeling changes were also included in the safety evaluation of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA and are reviewed in Section 7.6.1.6 and Section 7.6.1.7.
L609: ## 7.3. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarket Experience
L610: ## 7.3.1. Adverse Events Identified in Postmarket Experiences
L611: VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA is not currently approved in the United States or in any other foreign market. No postmarketing experience is available. Relevant information regarding risks identified through postmarketing experience of ELX/TEZ/IVA or medications that contain its components is included in Section 7.2.
L612: ## 7.3.2. Expectations on Safety
L613: Not applicable for this review.
L614: ## 7.3.3. Additional Safety Issues From Other Disciplines
L615: Not applicable for this review.
L616: ## 7.4. FDA Approach to the Safety Review
L617: All clinical studies conducted as part of the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA development program were evaluated for safety. Given the short exposure periods and smaller sample sizes in the phase 1 and 2 studies, the focus of the safety review is on the phase 3 development program, Trials 102 and 103.
L618: Both phase 3 trials included a 4-week ELX/TEZ/IVA run-in period followed by a 52-week treatment period. Given similarities between the trial designs and populations, the safety data from Trials 102 and 103 were pooled for analysis. The safety data were also reviewed from the trials individually, and differences are noted where applicable. For detailed summaries of the trial protocols, refer to Section 15.1.
L619: ## NDA 218730
## ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
L620: Subjects unable to tolerate ELX/TEZ/IVA were excluded from the trials. Although selecting the population this way was clinically appropriate for the trial design, it adds a significant limitation to the interpretation of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA safety data, as does the absence of a placebo arm for comparison of baseline rates. As such, the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA safety data were evaluated in relation to the ELX/TEZ/IVA treatment arms, as well as in the context of the known safety profile of ELX/TEZ/IVA. We are unable to draw conclusions regarding the safety of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA in patients who have previously been unable to tolerate ELX/TEZ/IVA, as reviewed in Section 7.7.1.
L621: The safety for pediatric subjects aged 6 through 11 years is extrapolated from the adolescent and adult data based on comparable systemic exposures and because CF is the same disease process across age groups. The pediatric safety data from the Trial 105 were also reviewed for the presence of additional potential safety signals; however, the lack of a control arm limits the overall interpretation of these data. As most of the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA exposure occurred in Cohort B1 of Trial 105, this will be the primary focus of the pediatric safety data review.
L622: All safety analyses were conducted in the safety analysis set, defined as all subjects who received at least one dose of trial drug. Unless otherwise noted, all AEs presented are considered treatment emergent adverse events, defined as AEs that started or worsened in severity on or after the first dose of trial medication in the treatment period up until 28 days after the last dose of the trial drug or completion of trial participation date, whichever occurred first. Adverse events that occurred in the run-in period or after the treatment period are reviewed in Section 17.
L623: No major data quality or integrity issues were identified that would preclude a safety review for this NDA. There were no major issues identified with respect to recording, coding, and characterizing AEs. The Applicant's translations of verbatim terms into the Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities preferred terms for the events reported in the trials were reviewed and found to be acceptable. The definition, grading scheme, and assessment of causality for all AEs and serious adverse event (SAEs) were reviewed and found to be acceptable.

L653: ## 7.6.1.2. Deaths, Pooled Analyses, Trials 102 and 103
L654: There were no reported deaths attributable to adverse events during the treatment period in the pooled phase 3 safety database. There were two deaths in Trial 103, summarized below, that
L655: occurred after the completion of the treatment period. Both subjects were randomized to the ELX/TEZ/IVA treatment arm.
L656: - Subject (b) (6) was a 39-year-old White female with CF complicated by CF-related diabetes and bronchiectasis, asthma, and a history of reported alcohol use/abuse that was in remission at the time of trial enrollment. The subject was on ELX/TEZ/IVA prior to trial enrollment. The trial drug was discontinued on Day 354 for an SAE of distal intestinal obstruction syndrome. In the weeks prior to this, the subject experienced AEs of moderate vomiting and liver function test (LFT) elevations and underwent a magnetic resonance cholangiopancreatography that demonstrated moderate hepatic steatosis, cholelithiasis with gallbladder wall edema or thickening, and an intraductal papillary mucinous neoplasm of the pancreas. The treating hepatologist's impression was that the changes were related to alcoholic steatosis and hepatitis. The subject then experienced pneumonia and a CF pulmonary exacerbation. During this time, commercial ELX/TEZ/IVA was restarted but discontinued again as the subject's LFTs remained elevated. The subject's clinical status continued to deteriorate and progressed to respiratory failure and septic shock. The subject's family decided to withdraw care, and the subject died. The reported immediate cause of death was acute respiratory distress syndrome from pneumonia with liver and renal failure listed as other significant contributing factors.
L657: The data provided do not suggest that this subject's death was attributable to ELX/TEZ/IVA; however, a component of drug contribution to progressive liver failure cannot be excluded.
L658: - Subject (b) (6) 52-year-old White man with CF complicated by pancreatic insufficiency, abdominal pain, and anterior liver nodularity. The subject had not previously received ELX/TEZ/IVA or other similar medication. Trial drug was discontinued on Day 184 for the elevated LFTs. The subject reported 'regular' alcohol use at screening (12 beers per week), which increased because of reported social events. On Day 211, the subject experienced an SAE of hepatic cirrhosis and severe portal hypertension. The following week, the subject was reported to have moderate portal vein thrombosis and a liver mass near the pancreatic head on CT. Subsequent biopsy revealed severe adenocarcinoma of the pancreas. The subject was discharged to home with palliative care and died a few weeks later.
L659: We agree with the Applicant's assessment that the cause of death was not likely related to trial drug.

L666: There was a numerically higher proportion of subjects (81, 16.5%) in the ELX/TEZ/IVA treatment arm than in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment arm (68, 14.2%) who experienced an SAE during the treatment period. The most common SAE reported was related to infective pulmonary exacerbation of CF. This finding is not unexpected as pulmonary exacerbations are a common complication of CF and is reviewed in more detail in Section 7.6.1.5.
L667: Influenza was the next most common SAE and occurred in a slightly higher proportion of subjects in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment arm (7, 1.5%) compared to those treated with ELX/TEZ/IVA (3, 0.6%). To evaluate this further, the SAEs were also analyzed by system organ class (SOC) (data not shown). The Infections and Infestations SOC was generally balanced between treatment arms – VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA (50, 10.2%), ELX/TEZ/IVA (46, 9.6%). There were also no risk differences calculated for any SOC or individual preferred term (PT) for which the 95% confidence interval excluded the null. Additional review of SAEs related to FDA Medical Query and time to SAE onset did not reveal any additional concerning safety signals.
L668: All SAE narratives were reviewed. The general rates of SAEs in the pooled phase 3 trials were similar to the treatment arm in Trial VX17-445-102 of the phase 3 development for program ELX/TEZ/IVA. The rates of SAEs in Trials 102 and 103 were generally balanced between trials. The small imbalance in SAEs between the pooled treatment arms is unlikely to be of clinical
L669: significance. Additional review of hepatotoxicity, suicidal ideation, infective pulmonary exacerbation of CF, and influenza is included in Section 7.6.1.5 and Section 7.6.1.6.
L670: ## 7.6.1.4. Adverse Events Leading to Treatment Discontinuation, Pooled Analyses, Trials 102 and 103
L671: The AEs leading to treatment discontinuation or interruption that occurred in \ge 2 subjects are reviewed in Table 40. The rates of AEs leading to discontinuation were balanced between the treatment arms; however, as reviewed in more detail in Section 7.6.1.6 and Section 7.7.2, a numerically higher proportion of subjects in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment arm discontinued the trial drug because of abnormalities in LFTs.

L675: The proportion of subjects with an AE requiring trial drug interruption was numerically higher in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment arm occurring in 19 (4.0%) versus 12 (2.4%) subjects in the ELX/TEZ/IVA treatment arms. This difference is partially driven by LFT elevations and rashes (reviewed in Section 7.6.1.6). There were no other concerning signals noted in the review of PTs leading to drug interruption or discontinuation. Overall drug discontinuation rates were similar between arms. The clinical significance of the imbalance in drug interruptions is unclear.
L676: ## 7.6.1.5. Adverse Events, Pooled Analyses, Trials 102 and 103
L677: The summary of AEs occurring in \ge 10\% of subjects in the pooled phase 3 trials is included in Table 41. As noted above, the majority of subjects (&gt;95\%) experienced an AE. This proportion of subjects with AE is similar to other CF clinical development programs including the phase 3 trials used to support the registration of ELX/TEZ/IVA.

L686: Many of the most common AEs reported are consistent with the known safety profile of ELX/TEZ/IVA and expected event rates in the CF population. The most common AE reported was infective pulmonary exacerbation of CF. There was a numerically higher proportion of subjects in the ELX/TEZ/IVA treatment arm (158, 32.2%) with this AE compared to the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment arm (133, 27.7%), although the risk difference did not exclude the null (risk difference [95% CI]: -4.5 [-10.2, 1.3]). Pulmonary exacerbations of CF are common in the CF population and were also recorded as other secondary efficacy endpoints. The analysis of the rate differences of pulmonary exacerbations of CF as an efficacy endpoint in both phase 3 trials was supportive of the noninferiority of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. The clinical significance of the imbalance in reported AEs related to infective pulmonary exacerbations of CF is unclear.
L687: The noninferiority design of the phase 3 trials and high background rates of AEs related to CF limit the ability to adjudicate adverse reactions related to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. To further evaluate the safety profile of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA, AEs occurring at a higher rate in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA arm were reviewed and are outlined in Table 42.

L695: The proportion of subjects with an AE related to influenza was higher in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment arm (52, 10.8%) compared to those treated with ELX/TEZ/IVA (26, 5.3%). Influenza was one of the few AEs for which the 95% confidence interval of the risk difference excluded the null (risk difference [95% CI]: 5.5% [2.2, 9.1]). As noted in Section 7.6.1.3, the rate of SAEs related to influenza was also numerically higher in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment arm. The significance of this difference is unclear. Additional PTs of upper respiratory tract infections and specific viral upper respiratory tract infections were also higher in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA arm; however, notably, coronavirus disease of 2019 (COVID-19) occurred at a higher rate in the ELX/TEZ/IVA arm (127, 25.9%) compared to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA (107, 22.3%). Overall, the viral infections observed did not contribute to increased rates of infective exacerbations of CF or hospitalizations. We also note that rates of AEs related to influenza were also elevated compared to placebo in the clinical development program for ELX/TEZ/IVA, but the clinical relevance of this finding was also considered uncertain at the time.
L696: Adverse events related to rash, transaminase elevations, and increased creatinine phosphokinase are discussed in Section 7.6.1.6. Other AEs reported, including cough, sinus congestion, abdominal pain, and nasal congestion, are often symptoms associated with CF. The interpretation of these, and other reported AEs such as headache and pyrexia, is limited due to the lack of a placebo arm to assess the baseline rate of these events in the trial population.
L697: We note the following AEs occurred at higher rates in the ELX/TEZ/IVA treatment arm with at least a 1% difference in the risk difference: infective pulmonary exacerbation of CF, COVID-19,
L698: sinusitis, vomiting, arthralgia, dyspnea, constipation, and back pain. The clinical implications of these differences between the treatment arms are also unclear.
L699: Review of the phase 3 trials individually and by SOC did not reveal any additional concerning safety signals. Because of the limitations from the trial design and high rate of background AEs related to CF, we recommend the inclusion of all AEs occurring in \ge 5\% of treated subjects occurring at a greater rate in VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treated subjects compared to ELX/TEZ/IVA be included in the USPI for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA.
L700: Overall, the review of common AEs did not demonstrate significant new safety concerns for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA beyond the known safety profile of ELX/TEZ/IVA. The analysis of the data suggests that the safety profile of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA is very similar to ELX/TEZ/IVA.
L701: ## 7.6.1.6. Adverse Events of Special Interest, Trials 102 and 103
L702: The following adverse events were prespecified as AESIs based on the known safety profile of medications similar to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA (e.g., ELX/TEZ/IVA) and safety information from the phase 2 development program: liver function test abnormalities, creatine kinase elevations, rash, cataracts, hypoglycemic events, and neuropsychiatric events.
L703: ## Liver Function Test Abnormalities and Related Events
L704: Transaminase elevations are a well described adverse reaction related to all approved medications that affect CFTR processing or function and have been included as a Warning and Precaution in the USPI of these products since the initial approval of IVA in 2012 (Vertex Pharmaceuticals 2012). During the course of the NDA review of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA, an FDA Amendments Act of 2007 safety labeling change dated October 22, 2024, was issued for the addition of a Boxed Warning to the ELX/TEZ/IVA USPI regarding the risk of drug-induced liver injury and liver failure attributable to ELX/TEZ/IVA. This change was based on data from clinical trials submitted to a recent supplemental NDA (refer to the supplemental NDAs 212273/S012 &amp; 217660/S03 uploaded to DARRTS on December 11, 2024 [DARRTS ID: 5493844]) and review of the available postmarketing data.
L705: Given the similarities between VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA and ELX/TEZ/IVA, the DILI team in the Division of Hepatology and Nutrition (DHN) was consulted to assist in the review of the potential DILI risk related to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. An Information Request was sent to the Applicant on July 16, 2024, to obtain additional information on cases of suspected DILI that were identified in the initial safety review. The DILI team within DHN also assisted the primary review team in the assessment of the individual cases and the risk for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA-related DILI was discussed at multiple internal meetings within the Division and Office of New Drugs.
L706: There were no cases of liver failure leading to death or liver transplant noted in the phase 3 pooled safety database for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. As noted, the review of the safety database is limited both by the lack of a placebo arm and the required exclusion of subjects intolerant of ELX/TEZ/IVA. There were five cases of nonjaundiced DILI that were assessed as ‘probably’ or ‘possibly’ related to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA, reviewed in more detail below. No subjects exposed to
L707: VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA had transaminase elevations with concurrent elevations in total bilirubin. One ELX/TEZ/IVA exposed subject did meet Hy's Law. Summaries of the cases assessed as probably related to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA are reviewed below:
L708: - Subject (b) (6) was a 48-year-old White man with CF and Gilbert's syndrome. The subject had been randomized to the ELX/TEZ/D-IVA arm during the treatment period of Trial 102 and continued to the open-label extension (Trial 104) and began treatment with VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. On Day 256 of Trial 104, the subject's transaminases were &gt;10\times the upper limit of normal (ULN) alanine aminotransferase [ALT] 813 U/L [14 x ULN] and aspartate aminotransferase [AST] 395 U/L [11 x ULN]) and the total bilirubin was 2.53 mg/dL (&gt;2\times ULN). The bilirubin elevation noted was of unclear clinical significance because of the subject's history of Gilbert's syndrome. The trial drug was discontinued the following day, and the subject withdrew from the trial on Day 307. Targeted laboratory evaluation that included hepatitis antibodies and antigens and evaluation for cytomegalovirus (CMV) and Epstein-Barr virus was unremarkable. An ultrasound revealed a liver of normal size and morphology. We note that the subject was also reported to have an AE related to a respiratory tract infection (viral respiratory tract infection by metapneumovirus) on Day 268.
L709: We agree with the Applicant's assessment that given the context of an unrevealing work-up and lack of alternative etiology, the elevation in the transaminases is related to hepatocellular injury due to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. We are unable to assess the clinically meaningfulness of the elevation in the total bilirubin because of the subject's history of Gilbert's disease. There is no evidence to support this event was related to the metapneumovirus respiratory tract infection.
L710: - Subject (b) (6) was a 40-year-old White man with CF complicated by pancreatic insufficiency who was receiving treatment with IVA monotherapy prior to enrollment. The subject was randomized to the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment arm. On Day 116, the subject had AEs reported for increased ALT and AST. The initial elevations in the transaminases were &gt;5\times ULN (ALT 371 U/L and AST 189 U/L) and the data available in the case narrative demonstrated that the transaminase levels peaked at on Day 120 (ALT 615 U/L [&gt;10\times ULN] and AST 318 U/L [&gt;8\times ULN]). Total bilirubin levels were within the normal range. Targeted laboratory evaluation that included hepatitis antibodies and antigens, evaluation for CMV and Epstein-Barr virus, and an abdominal ultrasound were unremarkable. The subject received the last dose of trial drug on Day 120 and withdrew consent and discontinued from the trial a few days later.
L711: The Applicant assessed that the role of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA in this case cannot be excluded. Additional information was requested from the Applicant on September 16, 2024, to further assess this case but the data available was limited as the follow-up clinical evaluation was done outside of the clinical trial setting. The provided data revealed that the transaminases continued to rise, with a highest ALT of 1130 U/L (&gt;28\times ULN) and a highest AST of 436 U/L (&gt;9\times ULN) and confirmed that the total bilirubin remained within the normal range; however, the data did not reveal a peak bilirubin. No additional follow up information was available. Our assessment of this case is that it is probably hepatocellular injury related to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA.
L712: ## NDA 218730
## ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
L713: - Subject (b) (6) was a 40-year-old White man enrolled in Trial VX23-121-012, a phase 1, randomized, open-label relative bioavailability, food effect, and dose proportionality of a granule formulation of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA in healthy adult subjects. The subject had a reported past medical history that included hepatitis A, parasitic infection (no other information available), and 'positive' CMV (no other information available). All LFT parameters were reported to be in the normal range at the screening visit prior to the first dose of the trial drug. On Day 20, six days after the second dose of the trial drug, the subject had AEs of increased ALT, AST, and gamma-glutamyl transferase reported. The ALT was 537 U/L (&gt;8 \times ULN), the AST was 219 U/L (&gt;4 \times ULN), and the gamma-glutamyl transferase was 269 U/L (&gt;3 \times ULN). The total bilirubin remained within the normal range. Subsequent follow up laboratory analysis revealed down trending transaminases. Targeted laboratory evaluation that included hepatitis antibodies and antigens and evaluation for CMV and Epstein-Barr virus was unremarkable; however, the available data are limited because no dates were provided by the Applicant. An abdominal ultrasound was notable for mild steatosis.
L714: The Applicant, in conjunction with their hepatology consultant, assessed that the subject likely had underlying nonalcohol steatohepatitis with an acute asymptomatic transaminase elevation that resolved after trial drug discontinuation and that an association with VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA cannot be excluded. Our assessment of this case was that, regardless of potential undiagnosed nonalcohol steatohepatitis, the acute transaminase event was probably hepatocellular injury related to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA.
L715: The overall assessment of the reviewed cases was that, although there were no cases of concurrent transaminitis and bilirubin elevation, the available data supports that there is a risk of hepatocellular injury associated with VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA.
L716: In addition, the maximum liver function test elevations from baseline occurring during the treatment period were evaluated in the pooled phase 3 safety database and are outlined below in **Table 43**. The available data to assess for maximum LFT elevations from baseline was limited by the exclusion of subjects with a history of intolerance to ELX/TEZ/IVA. Note that &gt;85\% of the enrolled phase 3 population had documented prior use of ELX/TEZ/IVA or another medication containing its components. These limitations may lead to a rate of LFT elevations in the ELX/TEZ/IVA that is lower than that seen in the ELX/TEZ/IVA registrational trials or observed in clinical care, but we are unable to assess the impact of these limitation on the subjects exposed to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA.

L721: There was a higher proportion of subjects with transaminase elevations in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment arm compared to ELX/TEZ/IVA. When evaluating maximum elevations in either ALT or AST, 29 (6.0%) subjects in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment arm had transaminase elevations &gt;3 x ULN compared to 15 (3.1%) in the ELX/TEZ/IVA treatment arm. Similarly, six (1.3%) VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treated subjects had either ALT or AST elevations &gt;8 x ULN compared to one (0.2%) subject treated with ELX/TEZ/IVA. However, when evaluating for elevations in total bilirubin, a higher proportion of subjects (21, 4.3%) in the ELX/TEZ/IVA treatment arm had elevations in total bilirubin between 2 to 3 x ULN compared to subjects (8, 1.7%) treated with VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. No subjects treated with VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA had total bilirubin elevations &gt;3 x ULN, compared to 8 (1.6%) subjects treated with ELX/TEZ/IVA.
L722: The clinical implications of the differences between the rates of transaminase and bilirubin elevations between the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA and ELX/TEZ/IVA treatment arms are unclear, but the overall assessment of the available data is that VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA likely conveys a similar level of risk of liver injury to ELX/TEZ/IVA. An additional analysis of the shift in LFTs from baseline was unrevealing (data not shown).

L728: Similar to the analysis of the maximum postbaseline laboratory elevations, subjects treated with VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA were more likely to have transaminase-related AEs recorded (43, 9.0%) compared to ELX/TEZ/IVA treated subjects (35, 7.1%). In general, the transaminase related AEs were also more likely to be severe in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treated subjects: four (0.8%) versus two (0.4%) in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment arm and ELX/TEZ/IVA treatment arms, respectively. However, overall SAEs were the same between treatment arms: two (0.4%) VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA exposed subjects versus two (0.4%) ELX/TEZ/IVA exposed subjects. There were also more subjects (7, 1.5%) in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment arm with transaminase related AEs leading to treatment discontinuation than those subjects (3, 0.6%) treated with ELX/TEZ/IVA.
L729: The ability to interpret the clinical meaningfulness of the noted differences between treatment arms is limited by the absence of a placebo and the population limited to those tolerant of ELX/TEZ/IVA; however, the data support the conclusion that VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA conveys at least a similar risk of liver injury to ELX/TEZ/IVA. Additional assessment of the potential for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA induced liver injury including labeling recommendations is included in Section 7.7.2.
L730: ## **Creatine Kinase Elevations**
L731: Section 6 of the USPI for ELX/TEZ/IVA includes the adverse reaction of ‘increased creatine phosphokinase’ noting that the incidence of maximum creatine phosphokinase was greater in ELX/TEZ/IVA-treated subjects compared to placebo and that some cases were associated with treatment interruption or rhabdomyolysis. Creatine phosphokinase elevation was designated as an AESI in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA development program. Table 45 reviews the in-depth assessment of creatine phosphokinase elevations in the pooled phase 3 safety database.

L736: Overall, there were similar rates of creatine phosphokinase AEs between treatment arms; however, there was a small numerical increase in the overall number of increased blood creatinine phosphokinase AEs reported (VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA 43 [9.0%] subjects compared to ELX/TEZ/IVA 41 [8.4%] subjects). In addition, blood creatine phosphokinase elevations &gt;5 x ULN were higher in subjects treated with VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA (38, 7.9%) compared to those treated with ELX/TEZ/IVA (32, 6.5%). The majority of AEs were mild to moderate. There were two SAEs, one in each treatment arm, leading to trial drug discontinuation and adjudicated as possibly related to trial drug. Both subjects had previously experienced elevations of creatine phosphokinase while on ELX/TEZ/IVA that were attributed to exercise. There were no cases of rhabdomyolysis reported.
L737: It is difficult to assess the clinical implication of the rates of creatine phosphokinase elevations without a placebo, but our review suggests that VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA conveys at least a similar risk to ELX/TEZ/IVA for creatinine phosphokinase elevations. We recommend continued inclusion of this risk in Section 6 of the USPI for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA.
L738: ## **Cataract Adverse Events**
L739: Cataracts have been observed in subjects, particularly pediatric subjects, treated with IVA monotherapy and in juvenile rat toxicology studies with IVA. The risk for cataract is currently included in Section 5 of the USPI for all IVA containing medications including ELX/TEZ/IVA.
L740: Trials 102 and 103 required ophthalmologic exams at screening/baseline and at Week 24 for pediatric subjects &lt;18 years of age. Adverse events related to cataracts, including those occurring in subjects ≥18 years, are reviewed in Table 46.

L745: Overall, the rates of AEs related to cataracts were similar between treatment arms. We recommend the continued inclusion of cataracts in Section 5 of the USPI for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA.
L746: Note that the development of congenital cataracts occurring in infants born to women who had taken ELX/TEZ/IVA or other similar medications was evaluated as a potential postmarket safety signal in 2023 (SSID 1005079). As part of that review, the risk for congenital cataracts was determined to be negligible and no further action was necessary beyond routine pharmacovigilance. The two cases of AEs related to congenital cataracts were recorded in adolescent subjects receiving the trial drug themselves and are not related to this congenital cataract NISS.

L753: Source: Clinical reviewer and clinical data scientist. Software: R. adae.xpt.
L754: Abbreviations: AESI, adverse events of special interest; D-IVA, deutivacaftor; ELX, elaxacaftor; IVA, ivacaftor; N, number of subjects in treatment arm; n, number of subjects in subset; PT, preferred term; SAS, safety analysis set; TEZ, tezacaftor; VNZ, vanzacaftor
L755: Overall, the majority of rash-related AEs reported were mild and there were no SAEs or life-threatening AEs related to rashes in either treatment arm. Only one severe event was reported in a subject who was a 28-year-old White female with CF and a history of contact dermatitis and multiple drug hypersensitivity events, previously taking ELX/TEZ/IVA. The subject was randomized to the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment arm and reported a rash described as maculopapular on Day 8. The following day, the rash had spread to include the feet, legs, chest, back, arm, and face. Symptoms progressed to include dysphonia and the subject described a raspy voice, 'itchy' sensation in her throat, and lip swelling. She was then hospitalized for the SAE of hypersensitivity. This AE was likely related to the trial drug but appears consistent with the known safety profile of ELX/TEZ/IVA.
L756: There is a numerical increase in rash and other skin-related AEs in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment arm compared the ELX/TEZ/IVA arm (risk difference [95% CI]: 3.3% [-0.4, 7.1]). It is difficult to assess this safety signal in the absence of a placebo arm and in a population generally tolerant of ELX/TEZ/IVA. The imbalance in rash events between the treatment arms was most pronounced in the first 4-6 weeks of therapy. At Day 40 there were 29 subjects (6.0%) with rash events reported in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA arm compared to 10 subjects (2.0%) in the ELX/TEZ/IVA arm (data not shown).
L757: The clinical significance of the increased number of events or timing of events in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment arm is not clear. However, the data suggest that VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA conveys at least a similar risk to ELX/TEZ/IVA for rashes and support inclusion of relevant data in Section 6 of the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA USPI.
L758: ## Hypoglycemic Events

L764: The overall rates of AEs related to hypoglycemia events were low; however, they were numerically higher in the ELX/TEZ/IVA treatment arm (18, 3.7%) compared to the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment arm (8, 1.7%). The majority of AEs were mild to moderate, with one subject experiencing a severe AE in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment arm.
L765: In the targeted review of the blood glucose laboratory analyses (data not shown), the rates of hypoglycemia &lt;70 mg/dL were higher in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment arm compared to the ELX/TEZ/IVA arm (168, 35.0% versus 151, 30.8% respectively); however, the rates of more severe hypoglycemia (&lt;54 and &lt;40 mg/dL) were rare and balanced between treatment arms.
L766: The rates of fasting hyperglycemia ranging from 100 to 125 mg/dL were also elevated in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA arm (91, 32.4%) compared to the ELX/TEZ/IVA arm (72, 23.9%), although this was in contrast to the rates of random (i.e., not fasting) hyperglycemia (\ge200 mg/dL), that were more elevated in the ELX/TEZ/IVA arm (70, 16.5%) compared to the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA arm (70, 16.5%).
L767: The clinical implication of the imbalances of serum glucose levels is unclear, particularly with a small overall incidence of related AEs. Overall, these data do not suggest an overt safety concern at this time.
L768: ## **Neuropsychiatric Events**
L769: Baseline mental health disorders, including depression and anxiety, are elevated in patients with CF compared to their aged-matched peers. Some published data suggest that medications that affect CFTR processing or function can have a beneficial impact on the mental health of CF patients on a population level (Piehler et al. 2024). However, there have been postmarketing safety reports suggesting that there may be increased rates of depression, including suicidality, in some CF patients taking these medications. Psychiatric events are also currently being evaluated as a postmarketing safety signal (SSID #1005257), and neuropsychiatric events were included as an AESI for the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA development program to further evaluate this safety concern. The in-depth assessment of neuropsychiatric AEs is included in Table 49.
