L774: We note that the AE of anxiety occurred in a greater proportion of subjects in the VNZ/TEZ/IVA treatment arm (21, 4.4%) compared to ELX/TEZ/IVA treatment arm (10, 2.0%), and the 95% CI of the risk difference excluded the null (risk difference [95% CI]: 2.3 [0.1, 4.8]). However, there were similar rates of overall neuropsychiatric events in both treatment arms. The majority of AEs were mild to moderate; however, there were severe events, SAEs, and AEs leading to discontinuation in both treatment arms. There were no deaths or life-threatening events related to neuropsychiatric events reported.
L775: The narratives provided by the Applicant were reviewed and include episodes of depression and suicidality in both treatment arms. Most cases had confounding factors, which, in addition to the lack of a placebo arm, make it difficult to assess causality. These data do not suggest that VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA conveys an increased overall risk for neuropsychiatric events compared to
L776: ELX/TEZ/IVA. Overall, the relationship between these medications and neuropsychiatric events remains incompletely described. This safety signal will be further evaluated in the ongoing NISS (SSID 1005257). Currently, the data do no support inclusion in the USPI.
L777: ## **7.6.1.7. Review of Postmarketing Safety Signals in Similar Drug Products**
L778: The phase 3 safety data were also reviewed for signals from ongoing post marketing safety reviews or previous reviews for which labeling changes were issued (outlined in Section 7.2). Note that two open NISS review topics, hepatotoxicity and neuropsychiatric events, were designated as AESIs and are reviewed in more detail in the above Section 7.6.1.6.
L779: ## **Hypersensitivity Reactions (SSID 1004722)**
L780: A Warning and Precaution related to hypersensitivity reactions, including anaphylaxis, was added to the USPI for ELX/TEZ/IVA and other medications that contain one or more of its components as result of a safety labeling change issued on August 3, 2023. An evaluation of AEs based on PTs, SOC, FDA medical queries (broad and narrow) was performed. There were no overt cases of anaphylaxis or angioedema reported; however, there were multiple AEs related to hypersensitivity events in both treatment arms, including the case described in Section 7.6.1.6. We recommend that the Warning and Precaution related to hypersensitivity reactions be included in the USPI for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA based on these hypersensitivity AEs noted, the shared or similar APIs in both FDCs, and the overall similarity in safety profiles between VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA and ELX/TEZ/IVA.
L781: ## **Increased Intracranial Pressure Disorders (SSID 1005337)**
L782: A NISS was opened to assess for the presence of increased intracranial pressure disorders on May 16, 2024, and remained under evaluation at the time of this review. To assess this risk in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA phase 3 safety database, a review of potential PTs related to increased intracranial pressure disorders included in the nervous system disorders, vascular disorders, and eye disorder SOCs was conducted (data not shown). There did not appear to be any PTs directly related to increased intracranial pressure occurring in either treatment arm in the phase 3 development program for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. This signal will continue to be monitored via routine postmarket pharmacovigilance unless otherwise indicated by results of the ongoing NISS.
L783: ## **7.6.1.8. Laboratory Findings, Pooled Analyses, Trials 102 and 103**
L784: Analyses of clinical laboratory results including chemistry, hematologic parameters, and total cholesterol, consisting of changes in mean values over time, changes in individual subjects over time, and potentially clinically significant values were conducted. There were no clinically meaningful trends or mean changes from baseline observed. Postbaseline newly occurring or worsening potentially clinically significant values for hematology and clinical chemistry were infrequent and similar between the treatment arms.
L785: ## 7.6.1.9. Vital Signs, Pooled Analyses, Trials 102 and 103
L786: No clinically significant changes in mean vital sign values (systolic and diastolic blood pressure, heart rate, and respiratory rate) over time were identified in the pooled phase 3 safety database. We note an increase rate of diastolic blood pressure readings below 60 mmHg in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment arm (141, 29.4%) compared to the ELX/TEZ/IVA treatment arm (111, 22.6%); however, there was a similar proportion of subjects with systolic blood pressure readings &lt;90 mmHg. These readings do not appear clinically significant as there were very low rates of hypotension related AEs reported during the treatment period.
L787: Increased blood pressure is included in Section 6 of the current USPI for ELX/TEZ/IVA. Review of maximum blood pressure readings and AEs related to hypertension did not reveal a significant difference between the treatment arms; however, 90 (18.8%) of subjects treated with VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA had a systolic blood pressure reading over 140 mm Hg compared to 90 (18.3%) in the ELX/TEZ/IVA arm. We recommend that increased blood pressure be included in Section 6 of the USPI of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA.
L788: Overall, no significant new safety concerns were identified in the analysis of vital signs.

L827: The AESIs evaluated in Trial 105 were the same as the adolescent and adult phase 3 trials and included LFT abnormalities, creatine kinase elevations, rash, cataracts, hypoglycemic events, and neuropsychiatric events.

L842: ## Creatine Kinase Elevations
L843: There were two (2.6%) subjects with the AE of blood creatine phosphokinase increased. Both AEs were mild in severity and deemed related to the treatment by the investigator. On review of laboratory analyses, one (1.3%) subject had a creatine phosphokinase elevation &gt;10 x ULN.
L844: ## Cataract Adverse Events
L845: There was one subject (1.3%) with an AE of cortical cataract that was considered mild in severity, not clinically significant, and considered possibly related to the treatment by the investigator.
L846: ## Rash-Related Adverse Events
L847: There were four (5.1%) subjects that experienced a rash event during the treatment period of Cohort B1 of Trial 105. All were assessed as mild in severity.
L848: ## Hypoglycemic Events
L849: There were no AEs reported for hypoglycemia in Cohort B1 of Trial 105. On review of the clinical laboratory results, 23 (29.5%) subjects had a blood glucose result of &lt;70 mg/dL, although only 1 (1.3%) subject had results &lt;54 mg/dL. The clinical significance of this is unclear without a comparator treatment arm.
L850: ## Neuropsychiatric Events
L851: There were four (5.1%) subjects that experienced neuropsychiatric AEs that included aggression, anxiety, and initial insomnia. All were assessed as mild, with none resulting in treatment discontinuation or interruption.
L852: Review of the AESIs that occurred in Cohort B1 of Trial 105 did not reveal any new safety concerns.
L853: ## **7.6.2.7. Laboratory Findings, Trial 105**
L854: Analysis of clinical laboratory results including chemistry, hematologic parameters, and total cholesterol, consisting of changes in mean values over time, changes in individual subjects over time, and potentially significant values were conducted. There were no clinically meaningful trends or mean changes from baseline were observed. The interpretation of these data is limited by the lack of a comparator, but, overall, the assessment of laboratory findings was reassuring and did not reveal any new safety signals.
L855: ## **7.6.2.8. Vital-Sign Analyses, Trial 105**
L856: There were no clinically significant changes in mean vital sign values (systolic and diastolic blood pressure, heart rate, respiratory rate, and temperature) over time that occurred during the treatment period for Cohort B1 of Trial 105. Although limited by the lack of a comparator arm, no significant safety concerns were identified.
L857: ## **7.6.2.9. Subgroup Analyses, Trial 105**
L858: A safety analysis by key demographic subgroup, including gender at birth and region (U.S. and ex-U.S. sites) did not reveal any clinically significant differences between subgroups.
L859: An additional subgroup analysis by subject's weight-based dosing cohort at baseline (&lt;40 mg and \ge40 kg) was limited by sample size (baseline weight &lt;40 kg n=70, baseline weight \ge40 kg n=8), but did not reveal a clinically significant difference in the rates of AEs.
L860: ## 7.6.2.10. Pediatric (6 Through 11 Years of Age) Safety Summary
L861: The safety results from Trial 105 did not reveal new safety signals or identify imbalances in AEs to suggest differential risks for children compared to adolescents or adults. However, given the single arm design of the trial and the small sample size, interpretation of these results is limited. Safety for the 6-to-11-year-old CF population was, therefore, extrapolated from the adolescent and adult populations enrolled in the phase 3 development program. Treatment with VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA leads to comparable systemic exposures between populations, and there are sufficient similarities in disease process across age groups to support extrapolation. Such an approach is consistent with the development of related products (e.g., ELX/TEZ/IVA) for pediatric CF patients.
L862: ## 7.7. Key Safety Review Issues
L863: ## 7.7.1. Assessment of Safety in Subjects Intolerant of ELX/TEZ/IVA
L864: ## Issue
L865: Alternative therapies for CF patients who are unable to tolerate ELX/TEZ/IVA or a medication that contains its components is an area of unmet need for CF therapeutic development. However, to maintain clinical equipoise, the phase 3 development program for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA required a noninferiority trial design that necessitated the exclusion of subjects intolerant of ELX/TEZ/IVA. The available data do not assess safety and tolerability in the ELX/TEZ/IVA-intolerant population.
L866: ## Background
L867: ELX/TEZ/IVA and other medications that affect CFTR processing or function have contributed to many clinical benefits in eligible CF patients, and over 80% of the U.S. CF population are prescribed one of these treatments. However, there remains an unmet medical need for alternative CF therapies for patients who are not currently eligible based on *CFTR* genotype as well as for patients who are intolerant of, or with persistent symptoms despite treatment with, the currently approved medications. The CF Foundation estimates that approximately 3,000 patients, almost 10% of the U.S. CF population, are currently genotype eligible but not prescribed one of these therapies (CFF 2023). Although many reasons contribute to this fact, intolerance to therapy is a primary reason for discontinuation of treatment in these patients.
L868: The Applicant chose a noninferiority trial design with ELX/TEZ/IVA as the active control for the phase 3 development program of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. Given the magnitude of treatment effect and pervasive use of ELX/TEZ/IVA as the standard of care, the Division agreed to this design
L869: ## NDA 218730
## ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
L870: (reviewed in more detail in Section 6.2.2.1). The noninferiority design necessitated that subjects with a history of intolerance to ELX/TEZ/IVA be excluded. The disadvantages of this approach are the inability to characterize the safety and tolerability in the ELX/TEZ/IVA-intolerant population and the limited generalizability of the observed results.
L871: ## Assessment
L872: In general, the safety of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA and ELX/TEZ/IVA appear comparable. As reviewed in Section 7.6.1, there were similar rates of overall AEs, severe AEs, and SAEs. The most common AEs were also similar between the treatment arms, as were the overall rates of AESIs. There were no new safety signals identified. These findings are reassuring since we anticipate that VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA has the potential to replace ELX/TEZ/IVA as the standard of care. However, these similarities in safety profile also suggest that patients intolerant of ELX/TEZ/IVA may experience similar adverse reactions to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA.
L873: To further characterize the safety profile of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA, we performed additional subgroup analyses on subjects that had not previously been prescribed ELX/TEZ/IVA or a medication containing its components prior to enrollment. We note that this analysis aims to characterize the safety profile in treatment naïve subjects but will not characterize the safety profile in ELX/TEZ/IVA intolerant patients.
L874: The analysis of the subgroup of subjects not previously prescribed ELX/TEZ/IVA or its components is very limited by sample size, as this subgroup only accounted for 15% of total subjects. In addition, all subjects received ELX/TEZ/IVA for four weeks during the run-in period prior to randomization and were, therefore, not entirely treatment naïve upon starting the treatment period.
L875: Subjects in this subgroup had a baseline mean ppFEV1 of 71.0% and a mean baseline SwCl of 52.2mmol/L. They were more likely to have a non-F508del TCR variant (20% vs 1.8%) and less likely to have F/F or F/MF variants compared to non-naïve subjects. The safety results in this group are summarized in Table 56.
L876: **Table 56. Overview of AEs in Subjects Not Previously Prescribed CFTR-Directed Background Medicationa (Pooled SAS), Trials 102 and 103**

L881: Abbreviations: AE, adverse event; ALT, alanine aminotransferase; AST, aspartate aminotransferase; CF, cystic fibrosis; CFTR, cystic fibrosis transmembrane conductance regulator; COVID-19, SARS CoV2 2019; D-IVA, deutivacaftor; ELX, elaxacaftor; IVA, ivacaftor; LUM, lumacaftor; N, number of subjects in treatment arm; n, number of subjects in subset; PT, preferred term; SAE, serious adverse event; SAS, safety analysis set; TEZ, tezacaftor; VNZ, vanzacaftor; w, weeks
L882: In the run-in period, 74 (51%) of subjects in this subgroup experienced an AE over the 4 weeks with two (1.4%) subjects experiencing SAEs, one infective pulmonary exacerbation of CF and one rash. This rate is higher than the rate of AEs in run-in period in the overall population (395, 38.3%) reviewed in Section 17.2. The clinical significance of this is unclear given the limitations of the sample size and duration under observation. The most common AEs reported during the run-in period in treatment naïve subjects were consistent with the known safety profile of ELX/TEZ/IVA and similar to the AEs reported during the treatment period, which included headache, cough, and rash. Infective pulmonary exacerbation of CF was only reported in one (0.7%) subject, which is likely attributable to the short treatment duration of the run-in period. AEs leading to discontinuation in the run-in occurred in five (3.4%) subjects and included COVID-19, influenza, and rash. One (0.7%) additional subject had trial treatment interrupted due to rash.
L883: In the treatment period, the overall rates of AEs were similar between arms—VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA 60 (84.5%) subjects and ELX/TEZ/IVA 55 (85.9%) subjects. We note the proportion of subjects with AEs is lower than the overall population – 459 (95.6%) versus 469 (95.5%) subjects in VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA and ELX/TEZ/IVA, respectively (see Section 7.6.1.5). The clinical significance of this difference is unclear but may be related to underlying differences in baseline CF disease.
L884: There were more subjects experiencing AEs leading to discontinuation in the ELX/TEZ/IVA arm (4, 6.2%) compared to the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA arm (2, 2.8%). The AEs that led to discontinuation were all related to LFT abnormalities except for one (1.6%) subject in the ELX/TEZ/IVA treatment arm. The clinical implication of this is unclear, since in the overall population, AEs related to LFT abnormalities and leading to treatment discontinuation were higher in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA arm. In addition, the rates of LFT elevations in the subgroup of treatment
L885: naïve subjects were higher in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment arm compared to the ELX/TEZ/IVA treatment arm (data not shown).
L886: Overall, our assessment of the subgroup of subjects not previously prescribed CFTR-directed therapies supports the conclusion that VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA conveys a similar safety risk profile to ELX/TEZ/IVA.
L887: ## Conclusion
L888: The available data support that the risks of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA and ELX/TEZ/IVA are comparable; however, there are no data to characterize the safety of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA in patients intolerant of ELX/TEZ/IVA or medications that contain its components. The Division recommends labeling strategies to address this uncertainty. We do not recommend a limitation of use since the potential benefit of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA in this population is substantial. We expect that additional characterization of the safety comparability of these products may only be clarified in the postmarket setting. To guide patients and providers on the risks of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA in the ELX/TEZ/IVA-intolerant population, we will include a Warning and Precaution that clearly describes the absence of data in patients intolerant of ELX/TEZ/IVA and guides prescribers to weigh benefits and risks before initiating VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA in this population. In addition, as discussed throughout this review, the serious and well-characterized risks of ELX/TEZ/IVA will be retained in the USPI for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA.
L889: ## 7.7.2. Risk for Hepatotoxicity With VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA
L890: ## Issue
L891: Transaminase elevations are a well described adverse reaction related to all approved medications that affect CFTR processing or function and have been included as a Warning and Precaution in the USPI since the initial approval of IVA in 2012 (Vertex Pharmaceuticals 2012). Liver toxicity findings were also noted in the VNZ toxicology program (Section 7.1) and previously documented in the IVA toxicology program (NDA 203188). The risk for potential hepatotoxicity related to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA was a major focus of the safety review.
L892: ## Background
L893: The hepatotoxicity risks listed on the USPI for ELX/TEZ/IVA had previously included elevated liver transaminases with and without elevated bilirubin as well as liver failure leading to transplantation in one subject with underlying cirrhosis and portal hypertension. As discussed in Section 7.6.1.6, this warning was elevated to a Boxed Warning for DILI and liver failure as part of a recent safety labeling change, dated October 22, 2024. In addition, a NISS (SSID 1005364) was opened to include queries for liver injury data across all approved products containing at least one component of ELX/TEZ/IVA. This NISS is in the action phase pending finalization at the time of this review.
L894: ## **Assessment**
L895: To assess the hepatotoxicity risk of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA, LFT abnormalities were pre-specified as an AESI in the phase 3 development program, and results are reviewed in detail in Section 7.6.1.6.
L896: The rates of transaminase laboratory elevations and AEs related to transaminases were higher in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment arm than in the ELX/TEZ/IVA treatment arm (Table 57).
L897: **Table 57. Maximum Transaminase Elevation From Baseline During the Treatment Period (Pooled SAS), Trials 102 and 103**
L898: Maximum ALT or AST Elevation	ELX/TEZ/IVA	VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA
N=491
n (%)	N=480
n (%)
>3x ULN	15 (3.1)	29 (6.0)
>5x ULN	6 (1.2)	12 (2.5)
>8x ULN	1 (0.2)	6 (1.3)
L899: Source: Clinical reviewer. Software: JMP v15.0. adlb.xpt using SAFFL(Y), ONTRTFL(Y), PARAMCD, AVALC, MCRIT1
Abbreviations: ALT, alanine aminotransferase; AST, aspartate aminotransferase; D-IVA, deutivacaftor; ELX, elaxacaftor; IVA, ivacaftor; N, number of subjects in treatment arm; n, number of subjects in subset; SAS, safety analysis set; TEZ, tezacaftor; ULN, upper limit of normal; VNZ, vanzacaftor; x, times
L900: Although it is difficult to interpret this difference given that over 85% of the population had been treated with ELX/TEZ/IVA or its components prior to enrollment, these data suggest that VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA conveys a similar risk of liver injury to ELX/TEZ/IVA.
L901: We further assessed the rates of AST and ALT elevations in subjects that were not previously prescribed ELX/TEZ/IVA or its components (the subgroup described in Section 7.7.1). Elevations in AST or ALT 3 to 5 x ULN were noted in four (5.8%) subjects in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment arm compared to one (1.6%) subject in the ELX/TEZ/IVA treatment arm. In addition, one (1.5%) subject in the VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA had an AST or ALT elevation greater than 8x the ULN compared to no subjects treated with ELX/TEZ/IVA. Overall, these data further suggest that VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA conveys at least a similar risk of liver injury to ELX/TEZ/IVA.
L902: On review of the individual cases, there were five cases of nonjaundiced DILI assessed as probably or possibly related to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA, including a case in a healthy volunteer. Refer to DHN consult dated December 19, 2024, for details (DARRTS ID: 5499379). There were no cases of Hy's law or documented cases of liver failure attributed to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. However, the available phase 3 safety database of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA is limited to 480 subjects treated for 52 weeks. Although this scope is sufficient to determine the overall safety profile of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA for chronic use, it is unlikely to capture rare events, particularly when compared to the estimated cumulative exposure of ELX/TEZ/IVA of 160,240 person-years (see Section 7.2).
L903: Finally, we note that the etiology of the liver injury related to ELX/TEZ/IVA has not been established. In the ELX/TEZ/IVA clinical program, the trough concentrations of ELX, TEZ, IVA, and their active metabolites reported from six subjects who experienced hepatotoxicity were all within the therapeutic range (refer to the supplemental NDAs 212273/S012 &amp; 217660/S03 uploaded to DARRTS on December 11, 2024 [DARRTS ID: 5493844]). The mechanism of injury is likely an off-target effect, mediated by one or more components of ELX/TEZ/IVA. That VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA shares the same (TEZ) and two highly similar
L904: components (VNZ and ELX, IVA and D-IVA) components also supports that the risk for liver injury is comparable to ELX/TEZ/IVA.
L905: Given the recent addition of the Boxed Warning for DILI and liver failure to the USPI for ELX/TEZ/IVA, there were multidisciplinary discussions held between the Division and DHN and within the Office of New Drugs regarding the assessment of risk related to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA and how to convey this risk to prescribers. The Division will include a Boxed Warning for potential DILI and liver failure related to VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. This risk is appropriate for a Boxed Warning since it is not only serious and life-threatening, but also potentially preventable through risk mitigation strategies. This approach is consistent with the FDA guidance for industry *Warning and Precautions, Contraindications, and Boxed Warning Sections of Labeling for Human Prescription Drug and Biological Products — Content and Format (October 2011)* for serious risks for which the frequency or severity could be reduced by interventions (e.g., increased monitoring frequency, drug interruption guidelines). The guidance also reviews that there are instances when a Boxed Warning based on an anticipated reaction would be appropriate, as in this case.
L906: ## Conclusion
L907: There are no data to suggest that the risk of DILI with VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA is different from or lower than the risk with ELX/TEZ/IVA. The Division will include a Boxed Warning, consistent with that in the USPI for ELX/TEZ/IVA, in the USPI for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. The Boxed Warning will include recommendations for increased LFT monitoring frequency and duration, guidelines for drug interruption, and considerations for population selection. Additional information regarding labeling is included in Section 23. An enhanced pharmacovigilance strategy will attach to the approval of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA to further evaluate this safety signal in the postmarket setting.
L908: ## 8. Therapeutic Individualization
L909: ## 8.1. Intrinsic Factors
L910: ## Hepatic Impairment
L911: The Applicant proposed no dose adjustment of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA FDC tablet for CF patients with mild hepatic impairment (HI) (Child-Pugh Class A) (b) (4). However, the use of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA in CF patients with MHI is not recommended and should only be considered when there is a clear medical need, and the benefit exceeds the risk. VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA should not be used in CF patients with severe HI (Child-Pugh Class C). The review team agree that this approach is acceptable, and these recommendations will be included in the USPI.
L912: The Applicant conducted a reduced design dedicated HI trial (Study VX21-121-008) to evaluate a single dose of VNZ 10 mg/TEZ 50 mg /D-IVA 125 mg FDC PK in subjects with MHI (n=12) compared to matched healthy subjects (n=12). Results of Study 008 showed that the impact of MHI on TEZ exposure was insignificant, while the mean exposures of VNZ and D-IVA were
L913: ## NDA 218730
## ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
L914: lower in subjects with MHI compared to those in matched healthy subjects. The total exposure area under the concentration-time estimated to infinity (AUC_{inf}) of VNZ and D-IVA decreased 30% and 20%, respectively, in subjects with moderate hepatic impairment when compared to matched subjects with normal hepatic function. Similarly, the free drug exposure (AUC_{unbound, 0-inf}) of VNZ and D-IVA decreased 20% and 40%, respectively, in subjects with MHI when compared to matched subjects with normal hepatic function.
L915: Based on the hepatotoxicity of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA (refer to Section 7.7.2 and the PK results from the dedicated HI Study VX21-121-008, the clinical pharmacology review team agrees that no dose modification is recommended for CF patients with mild HI; VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA should not be used in CF patients with severe HI, and VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA should only be considered in patients with MHI (without dose adjustment) when there is a clear medical need and the benefit exceeds the risk. Because of hepatotoxicity, the clinical pharmacology review team also agrees liver function tests should be closely monitored in HI patients on VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA treatment.
L916: ## **Renal Impairment**
L917: VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA FDC tablet has not been studied in CF patients with any degree of renal impairment (RI) or end-stage renal disease. There were CF subjects with mild RI (n=126) and moderate RI (n=2) in the phase 3 trials. The Applicant proposed no dose adjustment for subjects with mild or moderate renal impairment. The USPI will recommend use with caution in people with CF with severe RI or end-stage renal disease. The review team agrees that this approach is acceptable.
L918: Renal excretion contributed minimally to VNZ elimination (0.5% of the total radioactivity was detected in the urine within 17 days postdose, as indicated by Study 004). Per TEZ/IVA labeling, the contribution of renal route in TEZ excretion is approximately 14% (mostly as M2 metabolite) and &lt;1% of the administered dose was excreted in urine as unchanged TEZ. This result suggests that renal excretion is not the major pathway of TEZ elimination in humans. Although a mass balance study was not conducted for D-IVA, the urine excretion of D-IVA is expected to be low based on in vitro comparison results with IVA (6.6% of IVA dose is excreted in urine). In addition, population PK showed similar exposure of VNZ/TEZ/F-IVA between healthy subjects and subjects with mild/moderate RI.
L919: Overall, these results suggest that renal clearance is likely to play a minimal role in the elimination of VNZ, TEZ, and D-IVA and support the proposed dosing recommendation.
L920: ## **Pediatrics PK**
L921: The proposed pediatric doses in children 6 through 11 years of age (i.e., VNZ 20 mg/TEZ 100 mg/D-IVA 250 mg QD for children weighing \ge 40 kg and VNZ 12 mg/TEZ 60 mg/D-IVA 150 mg QD for children weighing \ge 40 kg) were studied in Cohort B1 of Trial VX21-121-105 with 78 children.
L922: The comparison of pediatric observed C_{through} values and population PK-estimated steady state AUC values of VNZ, TEZ, M1-TEZ, D-IVA, and M1-D-IVA following the studied/proposed doses were generally within the range of those in adults and adolescents. For details, refer to Section 6.1.3.
L923: ## 8.2. Extrinsic Factors
L924: ## Food Effect
L925: ## Study VX21-121-005 (Study 005)
L926: The effect of food consumption on drug exposure was evaluated in Part B of Study 005. Study 005 Part B was a phase 1, randomized, open-label, crossover study with 3-period, 3-sequence design to evaluate the effect of food on the relative bioavailability of VNZ [Form D]/TEZ/D-IVA FDC tablet.
L927: The PK analysis dataset included data from 17 healthy subjects who received VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA FDC tablets in the three dosing periods: FDC Fasted, FDC Low-fat (approximately 400 to 500 kCal including 11 to 14 g of fat [approximately 25% of caloric content]), and FDC High-fat (approximately 800 to 1000 kCal including 55 to 65 g of fat [approximately 50% of caloric content]). The effect of food intake on VNZ, TEZ, and D-IVA PK after a single oral dose of the FDC tablet (VNZ [Form D] 20 mg /TEZ 100 mg/D-IVA 250 mg) under fed conditions compared to fasted conditions was statistically evaluated in Table 58. Administration of a single oral dose of the FDC tablet following a fat containing meal increased VNZ and D-IVA exposures up to 6-fold and 5-fold, respectively, compared to fasted condition. Food intake had no significant impact on TEZ exposures overall.

L933: ## Drug Interactions
L934: ## Effect of VNZ, TEZ, and D-IVA on the PK of Each Other
L935: The PK interactions between 10 mg VNZ [Form A], 100 mg TEZ, and 250 mg D-IVA (in separate tablets) were evaluated in clinical trial in healthy subjects (n=20), Study VX21-121-006.
L936: When coadministered with TEZ and D-IVA, the C_{max,ss} and AUC of VNZ was increased 48% and 54%, respectively compared to VNZ administered alone. When coadministered with VNZ and D-IVA, TEZ C_{max,ss} and AUC increased 29% and 35%, respectively, and M1-TEZ C_{max,ss} and AUC were increased 17% and 11%, respectively. Likewise, D-IVA C_{max,ss} and AUC increased 18% and 27%, respectively, M1-D-IVA C_{max,ss} and AUC increased 27% and 39%, respectively, when D-IVA was administered in TC with VNZ/TEZ compared to D-IVA administered alone.
L937: There is a concern whether this up to 50% increase of systemic exposures of VNZ, TEZ, D-IVA and their active metabolites may change the efficacy/safety profile of each API in the TC context. For VNZ, the dose ranging trial of VNZ (Study 101) was conducted in the TC context, and the dose ranging results directly supported the 10 mg VNZ [Form A] dose in the TC context to be used in phase 3 studies. For D-IVA, although Study 561-101 was conducted on D-IVA mono-component in CF subjects with gating variants, based on the flat dose-response relationship over D-IVA 150 mg to D-IVA 250 mg on ppFEV1 response observed in Study 561-101, it is unlikely that the observed increase in systemic exposure would contribute additional efficacy of D-IVA in the context of TC.
L938: ## Effect of Coadministered Drugs on VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA
L939: ## VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA as Substrates of CYP Enzymes
L940: VNZ, TEZ, and D-IVA are all extensively metabolized by CYP3A.
L941: In vitro metabolizing enzyme profiling results (Study N295) showed VNZ was stable when incubating with recombinant CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, and CYP2D6 (&gt;95% remaining at the 60-minute time point) as presented in Table 59. The compound exhibited a turnover of &gt;18% in CYP3A4 and CYP3A5 (43.2% and 81.2% remaining, respectively).

L958: In vitro study DM-059 showed that when incubating with CYP3A4 supersomes, the mean half-life of D-IVA and IVA was 8.1\pm0.2 and 5.5\pm0.1 hours, respectively.
L959: There were two clinical drug-drug interaction (DDI) studies (Study VX21-121-007 and Study VX18-445-006) conducted on VNZ (as mono-component) and D-IVA (in the context of ELX/TEZ/D-IVA), respectively, with itraconazole, a strong CYP3A inhibitor. VNZ exposure was increased (C_{max} by 1.55-fold, AUC_{0-tlast} by 5.55-fold, and AUC_{inf} by 6.37-fold) when VNZ
L960: ## NDA 218730
## ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
L961: was coadministered with itraconazole compared to VNZ administration alone (Study VX21-121-007). The exposure of D-IVA was increased by 1.96- and 11.1-fold for C_{max} and AUC_{inf}, respectively, in Study VX18-445-006. The DDI study (Study VX14-661-006) under the TEZ/IVA development program indicated that itraconazole increased TEZ AUC and C_{max} by 4.0- and 2.8-fold compared to TEZ administration alone. In addition, DDI study (Study VX13-770-017) under the IVA program indicated that moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor ciprofloxacin did not affect TEZ exposure.
L962: Study VX12-770-017 in the TEZ/IVA program showed that ciprofloxacin, a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor, had no clinically relevant effect on the PK of TEZ when coadministered with IVA.
L963: Based on the finding in Studies VX21-121-007, VX18-445-006, physiologically based pharmacokinetic evaluation, exposure-response relationship of VNZ and TEZ, and CYP3A4-related DDI evaluation of TEZ under TEZ/IVA and IVA programs, the Applicant proposed to reduce the dose of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA FDC when it is coadministered with CYP3A inhibitors as presented in Table 62.
L964: **Table 62. Dosing Schedule for Concomitant Use of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA With Moderate or Strong CYP3A Inhibitors**
L965: Age	Weight	Moderate CYP3A Inhibitors	Strong CYP3A Inhibitors

L966: Source: Applicant proposed labeling
Abbreviations: CYP, cytochrome P450; D-IVA, deutivacaftor; QOD, every other day; TEZ, tezacaftor; VNZ, vanzacaftor
L967: VNZ, TEZ, and D-IVA showed linear PK characteristics around the therapeutic dose. Therefore, the proposed dose reduction to 1/3 to 1/4 of the normal dose (Table 62) is expected to lower the exposures (AUC) of VNZ and D-IVA in patients on CYP3A4 moderate inhibitors (which increase VNZ and D-IVA AUC by 2- to 5-fold) within the range of patients on normal dose without taking CYP3A4 moderate inhibitors (Section 14.5). The extent of the proposed dose reduction of TEZ in the context of TC when coadministered with CYP3A4 moderate inhibitors (1/3 to 1/4) is greater than the approved dose reduction of TEZ in the context of ELX/TEZ/IVA (1/2 of the normal dose), which is expected to result in a lower exposure (Section 14.5). However, the dose-response relationship of TEZ was relatively flat over 30 mg to 150 mg range when coadministered with IVA in patients with F/F, as observed in the TEZ/IVA program (Trial VX11-661-101). Considering the availability of dosing strengths of TC and the unmet medical need of CF, the proposed dose reduction of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA when coadministered with CYP3A4 moderate inhibitors is reasonable.
L968: Similarly, the proposed dose reduction to ~1/10 to 1/14 of the normal dose (Table 62) is expected to lower the VNZ and D-IVA AUC in patients on CYP3A4 strong inhibitors (which increase VNZ and D-IVA AUC by 10.5- and 11-fold, respectively) within the range of subjects on normal dose without taking CYP3A4 strong inhibitors (Section 14.5). Thus, the dose reduction for VNZ and D-IVA in the context of TC when coadministered with CYP3A4 strong inhibitors is reasonable. The extent of the proposed dose reduction of TEZ in the context of TC when coadministered with CYP3A4 strong inhibitors (1/10 to 1/14) is greater than the approved
L969: dose reduction of TEZ in the context of ELX/TEZ/IVA (2/7 of the normal dose). However, based on the flat dose-response relationship of TEZ over 30 – 150 mg range (Trial VX11-661-101), the proposed dose reduction of TEZ when coadministered with CYP3A4 strong inhibitors is reasonable.
L970: The proposed pediatric dose adjustments for VNZ and D-IVA when coadministered with CYP3A4 inhibitors are generally reasonable as population PK-simulated results showed the AUC at steady state values after the proposed dose adjustment were within the reference range (Section 14.5). The proposed pediatric dose adjustment for TEZ when coadministered with CYP3A4 inhibitors generally results in a lower exposure compared to the reference range. However, this can be similarly justified by relative flat dose-response curve of TEZ observed in adults.
L971: VNZ and D-IVA are sensitive substrates of CYP3A. The exposure to VNZ and D-IVA was predicted to be reduced with concomitant use of strong or moderate CYP3A inducers. For example, simulations showed significant decrease in exposure to VNZ and D-IVA by 82% and 90%, respectively, when coadministered with rifampin, a strong CYP3A inducer. Moreover, carbamazepine, a strong CYP3A inducer, significantly decreased VNZ and D-IVA exposures by 57% and 76%, respectively. The exposure to VNZ and D-IVA decreased by 69% and 73%, respectively, when coadministered with efavirenz, a moderate CYP3A inducer. Therefore, coadministration of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA FDC tablet with strong or moderate CYP3A inducers is not recommended.
L972: ## VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA as Substrates of Transporters
L973: In vitro studies indicate VNZ is not a substrate of P-glycoprotein (P-gp), breast cancer resistance protein (BCRP), organic anion transporter (OAT)P1B1, and OATP1B3 (Table 63).
L974: According to the labeling of ELX/TEZ/IVA, TEZ is a substrate of P-gp, BCRP, and OATP1B1. Coadministration of itraconazole (CYP3A4, P-gp, and BCRP triple inhibitor) with TEZ increased AUC and C_{max} of TEZ by 4.0- and 2.8-fold, respectively, compared to TEZ administration alone.
L975: In vitro studies indicate D-IVA is a substrate of P-gp (efflux ratio of 6.7 at 1 \muM, which is similar to value 5.6 of IVA) and BCRP (net efflux ratio of 42.1 at 50 \muM, which is similar to value of 32.5 of IVA), but not OATP1B1 and OATP1B3 (Table 63).

L980: ## Effect of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA on Other Drugs
L981: ## VNZ
L982: The plasma Cmax of VNZ at steady state following the proposed VNZ 20 mg/TEZ100 mg/D-IVA 250 mg is around 1.35 \muM. That translates to about \le20 nM free Cmax plasma concentration of VNZ. In vitro results showed that VNZ did not inhibit CYP1A2, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, and CYP3A4, and showed weak inhibition of CYP2B6, CYP2C8, and CYP2C9 with the most potent Ki value as 14.2 \muM (for CYP2B6). The half maximal effective concentration value of VNZ on CYP3A4 mRNA induction was estimated ranging from 0.3 to 1.4 \muM, and an Emax ranging from 3.4- to 5.2-fold. Comparison of in vivo Cmax value and in vitro inhibition/induction values, the in vivo CYP inhibition/induction potential of VNZ is low.
L983: VNZ is a P-gp inhibitor with a half maximal inhibitory concentration (IC50) of 3.80 \muM when digoxin is used as substrate. The calculated induced gut/IC50 value is 35. Given that coadministration of VNZ with TEZ (a P-gp substrate) and D-IVA (a weak P-gp inhibitor) only increased TEZ Cmax and AUC by 29% and 35%, VNZ is likely having low potential to inhibit P-gp in vivo.
L984: VNZ is a BCRP inhibitor with an IC50 of 0.25 \muM when prazosin is used as a substrate. The calculated induced gut/IC50 value is 532, which is almost two magnitudes higher than the cutoff of 10, suggesting VNZ has a potential to inhibit BCRP in vivo. Therefore, a postmarketing requirement study to evaluate the effect of VNZ or VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA on PK of a sensitive BCRP substrate is recommended.
L985: VNZ inhibited OATP1B1- and OATP1B3-mediated atorvastatin uptake with IC50 of 45.1 and 61.7 \muM, respectively, in the presence of HBE medium which contained 20% human serum. The
L986: NDA 218730
L987: ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
L988: calculated IC_{50,u}/C_{max,inlet,u} (1.47 \mu M) is smaller than the cutoff value of 10. Therefore, the in vivo OATP1B1- and OATP1B3 inhibition potential of VNZ is low.
L989: ## TEZ
L990: Refer to the approved drug label of ELX/TEZ/IVA (Vertex Pharmaceuticals 2019), in vitro results showed that TEZ has low potential to inhibit all the major CYP enzymes. TEZ is not likely to induce CYP3A, CYP1A2 and CYP2B6. TEZ has low potential to inhibit P-gp, BCRP, organic cation transporter 2, OAT1, or OAT3.
L991: ## D-IVA
L992: Major CYP enzymes inhibition data for D-IVA, M1-D-IVA and M6-D-IVA are summarized in Table 65. In general, CYP3A4, CYP2C8 and CYP2C9 inhibition potencies are similar between IVA and D-IVA, M1-IVA and M1-D-IVA.
L993: Therefore, it is expected that the in vivo CYP3A4, CYP2C8 and CYP2C9 inhibition potential by D-IVA is similar to slightly higher than that of IVA because of the doubling of C_{max} value. Of note, in clinical studies, IVA did not significantly affect the PK of rosiglitazone, a model CYP2C8 substrate, and midazolam, a sensitive CYP3A substrate (Study 770-010 and Study 661-006).(b) (4)
L994: (i.e., D-IVA may inhibit CYP2C9; therefore, monitoring of the international normalized ratio during co administration of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA with warfarin is recommended). Other medicinal products for which exposure may be increased by VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA include glimepiride and glipizide; these medicinal products should be used with caution.

L1010: ## 8.3. Plans for Pediatric Drug Development
L1011: CF is a rare, orphan disease and is not subject to pediatric study requirements as defined under the Pediatric Research Equity Act (PREA). However, the Applicant submitted proposals for the inclusion of pediatric subjects within the clinical development program for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA, and a Pediatric Written Request was issued on December 6, 2023.
L1012: Following approval, the indication for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA will include patients aged 6 years and older. This is based on safety and efficacy data obtained from the phase 3 noninferiority trials, Trials 102 and 103, which enrolled subjects 12 to 17 years of age (reviewed in Sections 6.2.2, 6.2.3, and 7.6.1). Additionally, a pediatric PK and safety trial, Trial 105, is ongoing to evaluate the PK of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA when dosed in triple combination in CF subjects 1 through 11 years of age (inclusive) with at least one TCR variant in the *CFTR* gene. In this application, the Applicant submitted interim analysis results using data from subjects 6 through 11 years of age in Cohorts A1 and B1 to support the extrapolation of efficacy and safety in this population (reviewed in Sections 6.2.4 and 7.6.2). The remaining cohorts in younger CF subjects 1-5 years of age (Cohorts A2, A3, B2, and B3) are ongoing.
L1013: juvenile toxicology studies were not needed to support this application for subjects aged 6 years and older. In a juvenile rat toxicology study, vanzacaftor had no adverse effects at up to 58 (male)/28 (female) times the exposure at the MRHD. Juvenile animal toxicology studies for IVA and TEZ were previously reviewed under NDA 203188 and NDA 212273, respectively.
L1014: ## 8.4. Pregnancy, Lactation, and Females/Males of Reproductive Potential
L1015: ## Clinical Experience
L1016: The use of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA, ELX/TEZ/IVA, or medications containing their components during pregnancy or lactation has not been evaluated in well-controlled trials.

Additional details are available in Section 13. L1021: **Table 66. Nonclinical Data of VNZ Supporting Labeling on Fertility, Pregnancy, Lactation, and Pediatric Use**
L1022: Labeling Section	Nonclinical Data
8.1 Pregnancy	In pregnant rats administered VNZ during organogenesis, VNZ did not cause adverse effects to the fetus at maternal exposures up to 30 times the MRHD.
In pregnant rabbits administered VNZ during organogenesis, VNZ did not cause adverse effects to the fetus at maternal exposures up to 22 times the MRHD. The high dose of 70 mg/kg/day (71 times the exposure at the MRHD) produced maternal toxicity (i.e., mortality, abortion, decreased mean body weight or body weight gains) and was associated with findings of increased postimplantation loss, decreased live fetuses, decreased fetal body weight, increased kidney malformations, and increased incidences of skeletal variations. The effects observed at the high dose were related to maternal toxicity and so were not considered relevant to the human risk.

8.3 Females and Males of Reproductive Potential	In a rat fertility study, VNZ had no effects on male and female fertility and reproductive performance indices up to 19 (male)/30 (female) times the exposure at the MRHD.

8.4 Pediatric Use	In a juvenile rat toxicology study, VNZ had no adverse effects at up to 58 (male)/28 (female) times the exposure at the MRHD.

13.1 Carcinogenesis, Mutagenesis, Impairment of Fertility	In a pre- and postnatal development study in pregnant rats, no adverse effects were observed in the offspring exposed daily to VNZ from birth (in utero) through lactation at maternal doses up to approximately 18 times the MRHD.

L1027: ## 9. Product Quality
L1028: ## Approval
L1029: The Office of Pharmaceutical Quality review team has assessed NDA 218730 with respect to chemistry, manufacturing, and controls (CMC) and has determined that it meets all applicable standards to support the identity, strength, quality, and purity that it purports. As such Office of Pharmaceutical Quality recommends approval of this NDA from a quality perspective.
L1030: ## Drug Substance
L1031: The CMC information for two of the APIs, VNZ and D-IVA, is provided in this NDA (NDA 218730). For the remaining API, TEZ, the Applicant has fully cross-referenced their own approved NDA 210491. Four minor issues involving VNZ and D-IVA were identified via our original review of the NDA on September 13, 2024. However, the Applicant's responses on September 20, 2024, have resolved those issues. Thus, the NDA is currently recommended for approval from the API perspectives.
L1032: ## Drug Product
L1033: Vertex developed the drug product manufacturing process for an immediate-release, oral formulation. The process involves [REDACTED] as outlined in Section 2.3.P.3. A Quality by Design approach was utilized to identify critical quality attributes for efficacy and safety. In-process control and release testing was implemented to monitor these critical quality attributes and ensure consistent product quality.
L1034: The drug product specifications were established using a Quality by Design approach, identifying critical quality attributes and referencing international harmonization guidelines and United States Pharmacopeia chapters. Data from development, release testing, stability studies, and manufacturing, were used to establish the specifications. The established specifications ensure the drug product's identity, strength, purity, and overall quality throughout its intended 24-month shelf life.
L1035: The noncompendial analytical methods ensure the identity, strength, purity, and quality of the drug product at release and throughout its shelf-life. These methods have been validated according to International Council for Harmonisation (ICH) Q2(R1) guidelines.
L1036: The drug product is packaged in a thermoform blister consisting of [REDACTED] films with a lidding foil seal. All components comply with relevant regulations, including 21 CFR 174-186 and USP. Container closure system components, including suppliers and specifications, are detailed in the referenced Drug Master Files [REDACTED] and [REDACTED]. The blister packaging passed the ICH Q1B photostability test, ensuring light protection for the tablets throughout their shelf life. Additionally, the proposed container closure system design itself has been previously approved for similar drug products from this Applicant.
L1037: The Applicant submitted batch analysis data for the drug product, encompassing five clinical/stability batches of the higher strength (VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA 10/50/125 mg) tablets and three batches of the lower strength (VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA 4/20/50 mg) tablets. All batches were
L1038: ## NDA 218730
## ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
L1039: manufactured using the proposed commercial manufacturing process (Section 3.2.P.3.3) and met the drug product specifications, both those used at the time of testing and those proposed for the commercial drug product.
L1040: A stability study, following ICH guidelines was conducted on five clinical/stability batches of the 10/50/125 mg tablets and three batches of the 4/20/50 mg tablets. These batches were evaluated under various storage conditions: long-term (25^{\circ}\text{C} \pm 2^{\circ}\text{C}, 60% relative humidity (RH) \pm 5\% for 48 months), intermediate (30^{\circ}\text{C} \pm 2^{\circ}\text{C}/75% RH \pm 5\% for 48 months) and accelerated (40^{\circ}\text{C} \pm 2^{\circ}\text{C}, 75% RH \pm 5\% for 6 months). After 12-18 months of long-term storage and 6 months of accelerated storage, no significant trends were observed in any of the evaluated product attributes.
L1041: **Shelf-Life:** Based on the satisfactory long-term and accelerated stability data, an expiration dating period of 24 months is proposed and granted when stored in the proposed container closure system. The drug product should be stored at 20^{\circ} to 25^{\circ}\text{C} (68^{\circ} to 77^{\circ}\text{F}) with excursions permitted from 15^{\circ}\text{C} to 30^{\circ}\text{C} (59^{\circ}\text{F} to 86^{\circ}\text{F}).

L1051: ## Biopharmaceutics
L1052: From a biopharmaceutics perspective, the application is adequate, and approval is recommended. The to-be-marketed and phase 3 formulations were the same so there was no need for bridging. Based on information provided, the Applicant considers the VNZ and TEZ to be Biopharmaceutical Classification System Class 2 (low solubility/high permeability). D-IVA is considered to be either Biopharmaceutical Classification System Class 2 or 4, as permeability studies were inconclusive. No biowaiver has been requested and both strength combinations were used in the phase 3 clinical study. The Applicant has provided data demonstrating that the dissolution methods are sensitive to material attributes, minor changes in the tablets' composition, as well as variation in the process parameters that could influence the release of VNZ, TEZ, and D-IVA. The methods are considered to be suitable for regulatory purposes. The Applicant has tightened the dissolution acceptance criteria for VNZ and TEZ to be reflective of the associated data and all dissolution acceptance criteria are now adequate.

There are only minor labeling deficiencies from the CMC perspective, and we expect the Applicant will accept our recommendations. Refer to the CMC sections of the final approved labeling.

L1064: ## III. Additional Analyses and Information
L1065: ## 12. Summary of Regulatory History
L1066: ## Tezacaftor (TEZ, VX-661)
L1067: Tezacaftor was approved on February 12, 2018, as part of new drug application (NDA) 210491 (TEZ/IVA). Vertex submitted a new molecular entity NDA on June 28, 2017, for TEZ/IVA combination therapy “for the treatment for patients with cystic fibrosis (CF) aged 12 years and older who are homozygous for the *F508del* (F) mutation or who have at least one mutation in the cystic fibrosis transmembrane conductance regulator (*CFTR*) gene that is responsive to TEZ/IVA based on in vitro data and/or clinical evidence.”
L1068: ## Deutivacaftor (D-IVA, CTP-656)
L1069: On December 22, 2015, a Pre-investigational new drug meeting request was received to discuss the plans for CTP-656, a deuterated isotopolog of ivacaftor (IVA) for use in CF patients. In the meeting minutes dated March 11, 2016, the Division provided clinical and clinical pharmacology comments regarding the development program, the comparator arms and wash-out period and stated that the proposed phase 2 study was insufficient to support phase 3 dosing. The Division also noted that the extent of the nonclinical and clinical bridge between D-IVA and IVA was unclear and the need for additional nonclinical studies would be determined by the adequacy of the bridging program.
L1070: On November 18, 2016, investigational new drug (IND) 128544 was submitted, for D-IVA, as a once-daily dosing treatment for patients with CF. The submission included phase 1 studies: a single ascending dose study, a multiple ascending dose study, and a bioavailability/food effect study conducted in Australia. The first study under the IND was a phase 2 study in which three doses of D-IVA, placebo, or open-label KALYDECO (IVA) were administered for 28 days. The study was deemed safe to proceed on December 23, 2016, with nonhold comments.
L1071: Vertex submitted a chemistry, manufacturing, and controls (CMC) Type C meeting request on August 14, 2017, for D-IVA, being developed in combination with TEZ and either VX-659 or ELX, to discuss an approach to leveraging existing CMC information for IVA to support development of D-IVA. Meeting Minutes were issued on October 18, 2017.
L1072: On September 19, 2018, the Division provided feedback for the submission received on July 24, 2018, on the proposed design of a phase 2 study evaluating a range of doses for D-IVA and the commercial dose of IVA. The comments discussed concerns that the proposed dose ranging study alone would not be sufficient to support phase 3 dosing.
L1073: ## Vanzacaftor
L1074: On April 10, 2019, IND 142001 for vanzacaftor (VNZ), a new molecular entity, was submitted with the proposed protocol, VX18-121-101 (Study 101) entitled “*A Phase 2, Randomized, Double-blind, Controlled Study to Evaluate the Safety and Efficacy of VX-121 [VNZ]*”
L1075: ## NDA 218730
## ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
L1076: *Combination Therapy in Subjects Aged 18 Years and Older With Cystic Fibrosis.*" The submission also included a request for Fast Track designation. The IND was granted Fast Track designation and deemed safe to proceed on May 30, 2019.
L1077: Rare Pediatric Disease Designation was granted on October 1, 2020 (RPD-2020-485). On October 26, 2020, Orphan Designation (DRU-2020-772) was granted for VNZ for the treatment of CF.
L1078: A Type B end-of-phase 2 meeting request dated December 9, 2020, was submitted to gain agreement on the proposed pivotal clinical development program and to discuss the adequacy of the nonclinical, clinical, and clinical pharmacology data to support initiation of phase 3 studies, as well as an NDA submission for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. In the meeting minutes dated March 26, 2021, the following discussions occurred:
L1079: - The Division advised Vertex to conduct a dedicated crossover pharmacokinetic (PK) study with four treatment arms (VNZ, TEZ, D-IVA, and VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA) to evaluate the PK interaction potential among the three mono-components before initiation of phase 3 trials, further noting that evaluation of the drug-drug interactions (DDIs) between individual components of a combination product is a required step to satisfy the "combination rule" (21 CFR 300.50).
- The Division raised a concern for the proposed dose for D-IVA, noting the current safety database for D-IVA was limited, and safety for D-IVA should not be borrowed from the IVA program. The Division strongly advised that the safety database should consist of 52 week-controlled data for more complete evaluation of safety.
- The Division acknowledged the justification for noninferiority margin using a meta-analytic approach and noted that substitution of D-IVA for IVA adds uncertainty for indirect comparison. The Division recommend that Vertex submit further biological or clinical justification for the proposed margin.
L1080: On April 26, 2021, a Type C meeting request was submitted to discuss Vertex's approach in demonstrating the contribution of the individual components of the fixed-dose combination therapy VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA and the design of 3-way crossover study to evaluate the PK interaction potential among VNZ, TEZ/D-IVA, and VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. The Preliminary Comments issued on August 12, 2021, noted that final determination of satisfying the combination rule would depend on the strength of the D-IVA to IVA bridge and that although the proposed 3-period crossover study design for the PK DDI study for individual components was reasonable, the waiver of a DDI study between TEZ and D-IVA should be sufficiently justified. On July 30, 2021, the Division also provided comments on the phase 3 trial protocols submitted on July 8, 2021.
L1081: Breakthrough designation request was received on May 27, 2021, and was denied on July 20, 2021, because the data with VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA was limited by small sample size, short study duration, and lack of concurrent standard of care comparison and because the data did not show a substantial improvement over existing therapies.
L1082: On October 7, 2021, a Type C meeting request was submitted to discuss the planned pediatric development program, seeking agreement on the proposed study design and the data to support

In a WRO issued on July 11, 2022, the Division provided comments on the proposal to leverage in vitro Fischer Rat Thyroid (FRT) data to support inclusion of *CFTR* variants in the prescribing information for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA and stated that the final determination would be a review issue.

In addition, the application will need to include any clinical or in vitro data in its entirety and a summary table of all *CFTR* variant indications, organized by clinically meaningful groupings, which clearly outlines the origin of primary data to support an indication, as well as any complementary data. The Division also did not agree to the submission of the proposed FRT data during the review period.
L1091: On May 2, 2024, Vertex submitted an NDA for VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA, “for the treatment of CF in patients aged 6 years and older who have a least one *F508del* mutation or another responsive mutation in the *CFTR* gene.”
L1092: ## 13. Pharmacology Toxicology
L1093: ## 13.1. Summary Review of Studies Submitted With the Investigational New Drug Application
L1094: The Applicant has conducted a comprehensive program of pharmacology, pharmacokinetic, and toxicology studies to support clinical development and the NDA submission of VNZ/TEZ/D-IVA. VNZ is the only component of this triple combination (TC) for which a complete nonclinical program was needed. All pivotal toxicology studies of VNZ have been submitted and reviewed previously under IND 142001. The Applicant performed a bridging study between D-IVA and IVA to leverage existing IVA nonclinical data. The Applicant has also provided combination toxicology studies for the new TC. The impurities studies have been submitted and reviewed under this application. The key findings from the nonclinical studies are cited and summarized in this review to support the approval of NDA 218730.
L1095: ## 13.1.1. Pharmacology
L1096: Primary pharmacology studies using human bronchial epithelial (HBE) cells from CF donors demonstrated that VNZ was able to improve CFTR glycosylation which would be expected to improve CFTR trafficking to the membrane. VNZ alone did not greatly improve CFTR function as evaluated by chloride (Cl) transport. However, VNZ in combination with TEZ and IVA greatly enhanced CFTR activity. VNZ did not appear to act as a potentiator for CFTR. Secondary pharmacology studies found that VNZ inhibits human prostanoid EP4 (K_i 0.64 \muM), IP (PGI2) (K_i 0.83 \muM), prostanoid EP3 (K_i 1.27 \muM), prostanoid EP2 (K_i 3.23 \muM), rat adrenergic \alpha_{1A} (K_i 3.48 \muM), and rat adrenergic \alpha_{1B} (K_i 7.46 \muM).
L1097: ## 13.1.2. Safety Pharmacology
L1098: Safety pharmacology studies indicated that VNZ does not affect the CNS or respiratory systems in rats at doses up to 10 mg/kg. In a good laboratory practice (GLP) human ether-a-go-go related gene assay, VNZ inhibited the channel with a half maximal inhibitory concentration (IC_{50}) of 0.7 \muM. The safety pharmacology study evaluating cardiovascular toxicity in dogs did not note any treatment-related effects. Additionally, the 4-week and 39-week repeat-dose toxicology studies in dogs evaluated electrocardiogram (ECG) parameters and did not find any treatment-related effects. Therefore, the results of the human ether-a-go-go related gene assay were not considered clinically meaningful.
L1099: ## Absorption, Distribution, Metabolism, Excretion/PK
L1100: Metabolite profiling using liver microsomes and cryopreserved hepatocytes of human, rat, dog, and monkey origin did not identify any human-specific metabolites. There were also no major metabolites identified in rats, dogs, or monkeys. Cytochrome P450 (CYP)3A4/5 appeared to be the major metabolic enzymes responsible for VNZ metabolism via oxidative metabolism. VNZ does not appear to partition into rat, dog, monkey, or human red blood cells. Plasma binding was high (&gt;99.9% bound) across rats, dogs, monkeys, and humans. Tissue distribution study using
L1101: male Sprague-Dawley rats given single oral doses of VNZ (3, 10, and 30 mg/kg), found little drug distribution to the brain, lung, heart, and kidney (tissue to plasma ratios ranged from 0.115-1.18 and did not increase with dose). Liver to plasma ratios were higher than all other tissues and decreased with increasing dose (17.3, 11.53, and 3.16, respectively).
L1102: ## 13.1.3. General Toxicology
L1103: ## Repeat Dose Toxicology Studies In Rats
L1104: ## 4-Week Rat Study (VRT-1725010-TX-006)
L1105: In a GLP study, Sprague Dawley rats (10 animals/sex/group) were administered VNZ by oral gavage once daily at 0 (vehicle), 1, 5, and 20 mg/kg/day in males and at 0, 0.5, 2.5, and 10 mg/kg/day in females for 4 weeks. The sex-specific dosing was due to preliminary studies showing that female rats had twice the exposure of male rats receiving the same dose. There was one low dose female that was euthanized on Day 7 due to shallow, difficult breathing. The cause of death was likely from technical error with the gavage delivery. Hematology noted a dose-dependent decrease in basophil counts in both males and females. Monocyte counts increased in a dose-dependent manner for both sexes. No potential target organs of toxicity were identified. The no observed adverse effect level (NOAELs) were identified as 20 mg/kg (AUC: 418 hr\cdot\mug/mL) for males and 10 mg/kg (AUC: 655 hr\cdot\mug/mL) for females.
L1106: ## 26-Week Rat Study (VX-121-TX-007)
L1107: In a GLP study, Sprague Dawley rats (15 animals/sex/group) were administered VNZ by oral gavage once daily at 0 (vehicle), 5, 12, 25, and 125 mg/kg/day in males and 0, 2.5, 6, 12.5, and 30 mg/kg/day in females for 26 weeks with additional animals included in the control and 125(male)/30(female) mg/kg/day groups for a 6-week recovery period. All male rats administered 125 mg/kg/day VNZ were found dead or euthanized by Day 14 of the study due to declining clinical condition. Most of the animals had erosions or ulcers in the nonglandular or glandular stomach. Four female rats in the main and toxicokinetic groups administered 30 mg/kg/day VNZ were euthanized or found dead on Day 98, 136 (2), and 183. A cause of death was not identified for all of the female rats but several had findings in the stomach. Target organs of toxicity were the liver (hepatocellular vacuolar degeneration for males at 25 and 125 mg/kg/day and multifocal hepatocellular necrosis for females at the 30 mg/kg/day), glandular stomach (erosion(s) and severe ulceration for males at 125 mg/kg/day and females at 30 mg/kg/day), nonglandular stomach (ulceration for males at 125 mg/kg/day, hyperplasia and/or hyperkeratosis in males at 125 mg/kg/day and females at 30 mg/kg/day), adrenal gland (hypertrophy for males \ge25 mg/kg/day and females \ge12.5 mg/kg/day), and decreases in lymphocytes in the spleen, thymus, and lymph nodes for males \ge25 mg/kg/day and females \ge12.5 mg/kg/day. The NOAELs were identified as 12 mg/kg/day (AUC: 390 \mug\cdothr/mL) for males and 12.5 mg/kg/day (AUC: 1120 \mug\cdothr/mL) for females due to mortality, reduced body weight, multifocal hepatocellular necrosis, ulcers and/or erosions in the glandular and nonglandular stomach for males at 125 mg/kg/day and females at 30 mg/kg/day, and hepatocellular vacuolar degeneration for males at 25 and 125 mg/kg/day.
L1108: ## **Repeat Dose Toxicology Studies In Beagle Dogs**
L1109: ## **4-Week Dog Study (VRT-1725010-TX-007)**
L1110: In a GLP study, Beagle dogs (4 animals/sex/group) were administered VNZ by oral gavage once daily at 0 (vehicle), 0.3, 1.0, and 4.0 mg/kg for 4 weeks. One high dose female was euthanized on Day 11 due to clinical signs of coughing, lacrimation, decreased activity, emesis with frothy white material that progressed to frothy red material, and red frothy vomitus. At necropsy, the lungs were discolored red which correlated microscopically with hemorrhage. There was also moderate edema and neutrophilic infiltration. These findings were consistent with aspiration of the test article and likely due to a gavage error. There were no adverse findings in the remaining animals at the end of the study. No potential target organs of toxicity were identified. The NOAEL identified as 4.0 mg/kg (AUC: 356 \mug\cdothr/mL, average of males and females).
L1111: ## **39-Week Dog Study (VX-121-TX-008)**
L1112: In a GLP study, Beagle dogs (4 animals/sex/group) were administered VNZ by oral gavage once daily at 0 (vehicle), 1.5, 4, or 10 mg/kg for 39 weeks with additional animals included in the control and 10 mg/kg/day groups for a 6-week recovery period. All dogs were sexually mature based on histopathologic assessment. No potential target organs of toxicity were identified. The NOAEL was identified as 10 mg/kg/day (AUC: 1960 \mug\cdothr/mL, average of males and females).
L1113: ## **Special Studies**
L1114: ## **13-Week Oral Rat Bridging Study for D-IVA**
L1115: A 13-week oral rat bridging study (VX-561-TX-001) was conducted to compare D-IVA at 0 (vehicle), 8.75, 17.5, 35, or 100 mg/kg/day to IVA at 100 mg/kg/day only. Unscheduled deaths in 19% of 100 mg/kg D-IVA and 11% of 100 mg/kg IVA animals resulted in early termination of both 100 mg/kg groups one week early. Target organs were identified as the kidney (basophilic tubules, tubular dilatation, and interstitial fibrosis), stomach (increased mucosal thickness), and thyroid (follicular cell hypertrophy). There were no toxicologically significant differences in clinical pathology, organ weights, or histopathology at 100 mg/kg/day D-IVA versus IVA. There were no adverse findings at the lower D-IVA doses. The NOAEL was identified as 35 mg/kg/day D-IVA. This study was judged adequate to establish a bridge to the ivacaftor nonclinical program to support the clinical development of D-IVA. No further general toxicology, developmental and reproductive toxicology, or carcinogenicity studies were recommended.
L1116: ## **13-Week Combination Rat Study (VX-121-TX-012/VX-661-TX-088/VX-561-TX-001)**
L1117: In a GLP study, Sprague Dawley rats (10 animals/sex/group) were administered 0 (vehicle), - VNZ (2.5 mg/kg/day) alone or in dual or TC with TEZ (45 mg/kg/day) and/or D-IVA (17.5 mg/kg/day) by oral gavage once daily for 13 weeks. TEZ- and VNZ/D-IVA/TEZ-related clinical chemistry (decreases in triglyceride concentrations) and urinalysis effects (increases in urine volume with concurrent decreases in urine specific gravity) were observed in males. No potential target organs of toxicity were identified. There were no additive toxicity effects when VNZ was administered in combinations with TEZ and/or D-IVA. The NOAELs were identified
L1118: ## NDA 218730
## ALYFTREK (vanzacaftor/tezacaftor/deutivacaftor) Tablets
L1119: as VNZ, TEZ, or D-IVA, alone at 2.5, 45, or 17.5 mg/kg/day, respectively, or in combination at 2.5/17.5 mg/kg/day (VNZ/D-IVA) or 2.5/17.5/45 mg/kg/day (VNZ/ D-IVA/TEZ).

L1127: ## 13.1.4. Genetic Toxicology
L1128: Vanzacaftor was negative in the Ames assay, in vitro micronucleus assay in TK6 cells, and in vivo micronucleus assay in male Sprague-Dawley rats.
L1129: ## Carcinogenicity
L1130: In the 6-month carcinogenicity study, transgenic rasH2 mice were administered 0 (vehicle), 3, 10, and 30 mg/kg/day vanzacaftor by oral gavage. The Applicant did have concurrence from the Executive Carcinogenicity Assessment Committee for dose selection. There were no treatment-related effects on mortality rates and body weights. There were no statistically significant treatment-related tumor findings in male or female mice. All animals in the positive control group (urethane) were observed with neoplastic findings. Non-neoplastic target organs of toxicity were kidney (multifocal unilateral mononuclear cell infiltration) and thymus (minimal hemorrhage).
L1131: ## Developmental and Reproductive Toxicology
L1132: ## Fertility Studies
L1133: In a GLP fertility study (Study VX-121-TX-009), VNZ was administrated to rats at oral doses of 0 (vehicle), 2.5, 5, and 12.5 (Male, M)/10 (Female, F) mg/kg/day (3, 9, and 19 (M)/30 (F) times the exposure at the maximum recommended human dose [MRHD], respectively). Males were dosed for 28 days prior to mating, throughout mating, and continuing until the day prior to euthanasia. Females were dosed for 14 days prior to mating, throughout mating, and continuing until Gestation Day 7. VNZ had no effects on male and female fertility and reproductive performance indices up to 12.5 (M)/10 (F) mg/kg/day (19 (M)/30 (F) times the exposure at the
L1134: MRHD). In conclusion, the NOAEL for fertility in this study was determined to be 12.5 mg/kg in males and 10 mg/kg in females associated with AUC0-24h of 361 and 565 µg·hr/mL, respectively.
L1135: ## Embryofetal Development Studies
L1136: In a GLP embryo-fetal development study (Study VX-121-TX-005), pregnant rats were administered VNZ at oral doses of 0 (vehicle), 2.5, 5, and 10 mg/kg/day (4, 10, and 30 times the exposure at the MRHD, respectively) during the period of organogenesis from gestation days 6 to 17. VNZ did not cause adverse effects to the fetus at exposures up to 30 times the MRHD. In conclusion, the NOAEL for maternal and embryo/fetal toxicities was determined to be 10 mg/kg/day (AUC0-24h, 565 µg·hr/mL) in rats.
L1137: In a GLP embryo-fetal development study (Study VX-121-TX-006), pregnant rabbits were administered VNZ at oral doses of 0 (vehicle), 10, 40, and 70 mg/kg/day (3, 22, and 71 times the exposure at the MRHD, respectively) during the period of organogenesis from gestation Days 7 to 20. VNZ did not cause adverse effects to the fetus at maternal exposures up to 22 times the MRHD. The high dose of 70 mg/kg/day (71 times the exposure at the MRHD) produced maternal toxicity (i.e., mortality, abortion, decreased mean body weight or body weight gains) and was associated with findings of increased postimplantation loss, decreased live fetuses, decreased fetal body weight, increased kidney malformations, and increased incidences of skeletal variations. The effects observed at the high dose were related to maternal toxicity and so were not considered relevant to the human risk. In conclusion, the NOAEL for maternal and embryo/fetal toxicities was determined to be 40 mg/kg/day (AUC0-24h, 410 µg·hr/mL) in rabbits.
L1138: ## Pre- and Postnatal Development Study
L1139: In a GLP pre- and postnatal development study (Study VX-121-TX-017), pregnant rats were administered VNZ at oral doses of 0 (vehicle), 2.5, 5, and 10 mg/kg/day (approximately 2, 7, and 18 times the exposure at the MRHD, respectively) from gestation Day 7 to lactation/postpartum Day 20. No adverse effects were observed in the offspring at maternal doses up to approximately 18 times the MRHD. On PND 14, AUC values of VNZ in dams were 10.0 to 28.6 times greater than that in pups. In conclusion, the NOAEL for maternal and developmental toxicities was determined to be 10 mg/kg/day (AUC0-24h, 339 µg·hr/mL) in rats.
L1140: ## Juvenile Animal Study
L1141: In a GLP juvenile animal study (Study VX-121-TX-019), juvenile rats were administered VNZ at oral doses of 0 (vehicle), 2.5, 10 (M)/6 (F), and 25 (M)/12.5 (F) mg/kg/day (approximately 4, 15, and 58 (M)/28 (F) times the exposure at the MRHD, respectively) from postnatal Day 7 through postnatal Day 70. No adverse effects were observed at oral doses up to approximately 58 (M)/28 (F) times the MRHD. Increased in white blood cell count, gamma-glutamyl transferase activity, total bilirubin, total bile acids, and phosphorus concentrations and decreased cholesterol and urea were observed in a dose-dependent manner in VNZ-treated groups. Without associated histopathological findings, these findings are not considered adverse. In conclusion, the NOAEL for juvenile toxicities was determined to be 25 mg/kg/day in males and 12.5 mg/kg/day in females, associated with AUC0-24h of 1100 and 532 µg·hr/mL, respectively.

L1145: ## 3.2.S.3.2 Impurities
L1146: The Applicant followed the recommendations of the International Council for Harmonisation (ICH) guidance for industry *M7(R1) Assessment and Control of DNA Reactive (Mutagenic) Impurities in Pharmaceuticals to Limit Potential Carcinogenic Risk* (March 2018) and evaluated the mutagenic potential of impurities based on literature, internal databases, and in silico assessment including Leadscape Genetox Statistical Models and Derek Nexus. Overall, the proposed impurity specifications are acceptable and there are no outstanding nonclinical safety concerns.
L1147: Other identified impurities are appropriately controlled per the ICH guidance for industry *Q3A(R) Impurities in New Drug Substances* (June 2008), ICH guidance for industry *Q3B(R) Impurities in New Drug Products* (August 2006), United States Pharmacopeia (USP) Guidelines or qualified in nonclinical toxicology studies.
